Code:

F&W ECOL 875 · Fall 2026

AI-Assisted Research Workflows

Over four sessions you take one research project through the whole cycle, from idea to shared result. Zotero keeps your sources, Obsidian keeps your thinking, and Claude works as a research assistant. You keep the judgment, the checking, and the authorship.

Meets once a week for 4 weeks, 3 h 45 min eachCredits 1 (15 h in class, 30 h outside)Instructor Min Chen · mchen392@wisc.edu
WEEK 1Setup, literature, questionsZotero, Obsidian, Claude Code in your vault, Research mode WEEK 2Experiments and analysisA worked carbon-model example, then your own data; skills, review agents, git WEEK 3Writing and reviewStory, editing passes in Word, citations, review agents, proposals WEEK 4Present, share, set your rulesSlides for three audiences, flash talks, a release, AI rules

Before Week 1

We install everything together in Week 1, and you write your project summary and do the prompt ladder in class. Before then, only: get a Claude Pro plan, turn off the model-training setting, and bring a laptop you can install software on. There is nothing to read beforehand. The list is under Week 1 → Before class.

Your anchor project

Everything in this course happens on one real project you choose: a thesis chapter, a planned study, or a dataset you need to analyze. Each week's work adds to a research record you keep. If you don't have a project yet, use the class project: How much of the year-to-year variation in burned area does fire-season aridity (vapor pressure deficit) explain, and does that hold across regions? It comes with three papers and a synthetic dataset of burned area in ten fire regions.

"Experiments" in this course are computational: no fieldwork and no wet lab. You analyze your own data, or the class dataset, under a written plan: Claude Code builds the analysis, tests and reviewer agents check it, you decide what to fix, and git records every version so that someone else can rerun it.

How each session runs

Every lab on this site is a step-by-step tutorial: each step says what to do, what you should see when it worked, and what to try if it didn't. Tick "Done" as you go; code follows the R/Python switch in the sidebar.

Every session follows the same rhythm, so you always know what's coming:

PartWhat happens
Setup checkConfirm your tools work before the labs start. In Week 1 the whole class installs and connects the tools together.
Framing and demoA short talk on one idea, then a live demonstration.
LabsHands-on work on your own project or the class project, with step-by-step instructions and prompts on this site.
Break-it exerciseA planted problem to find: wrong citations, a flawed script, bad data, a hidden instruction.
DebriefWhat surprised you, what Claude missed, and what to practice before next time. Weeks 1 and 2 end with a short exit ticket.

New to terms like agent, MCP, hook, skill, or vault? The Glossary explains each one in plain language and says where it comes up in the course.

Four habits, every week

Verify

Nothing an AI produces goes into your work until you have checked it against a source, a test, or your own expertise.

Log

Keep an AI-use log: tool, prompt, purpose, what you kept, and how you checked it. From Week 3 a hook writes part of it for you.

Disclose

Anything you write with AI help, in this course or in your research, ends with a specific disclosure statement. See AI rules and data.

Stay the author

AI may draft, suggest, and critique. You decide, justify, and take responsibility for every claim.

Grading

The course is graded Satisfactory/Unsatisfactory, based on attending the four sessions and taking part in the labs and the exit tickets of Weeks 1 and 2. There are no scored assignments. Between sessions you'll find suggested practices and suggested readings that carry each week's methods over to your own project; do what helps you.

The full syllabus, including university policies, is posted on Canvas.

Week 1 →

Week 1 · Research stage: setup, literature, and questions

Setup, literature, and questions

Zotero keeps your sources. Obsidian keeps your thinking. Claude helps with one bounded task at a time. You check the evidence and make the scientific calls. Today you install and connect all three, write a short summary of your project, and use the tools to go from three papers to a research question you can defend.

In this session

  • Setup A: Claude, Zotero, Obsidian
  • Lab 1: Project summary and prompt ladder
  • Lab 2: Citation audit
  • Setup B: Zotero connector and Claude Code
  • Lab 3: Read and annotate
  • Lab 4: Claude Code in your vault
  • Lab 5: Research question

Before class

There's nothing to read or write before the first class. You install the software, read the papers, and write your project summary in class, together. Before class, sort out only the things that need your account or your laptop.

Cost

A Claude Pro plan is required (about $20 per month). Research mode, Cowork, and Claude Code aren't on the free plan. If cost is a barrier, email the instructor privately before Week 1. Zotero (with its free 300 MB of sync storage) and Obsidian are free.

Why the training setting matters

On personal plans, your chats can be used to train future models unless you turn this off, and chats you allow for training are kept much longer (Anthropic says up to five years, against 30 days otherwise).

The class project

All four weeks use one shared project, so everyone can check everyone else's work. You can switch to your own project whenever its literature or data are ready.

Class question

How much of the year-to-year variation in burned area does fire-season aridity, measured as vapor pressure deficit (VPD), explain, and does that relationship hold across regions?

This week you read three papers that answer parts of it in very different ways. In Week 2 you analyze data: your own, or a class dataset of burned area in ten fire regions.

Key idea: who does what

Three tools can easily become three places to keep the same information, half of it out of date. Give each one a single job.

ToolIts jobMain outputNot its job
ZoteroBibliographic records, the PDFs, your highlights and comments, and later the citations in your manuscriptA library you trustHolding references an AI suggested that you haven't checked
ObsidianYour notes on each paper, comparisons across papers, questions, decisionsA research notebook that outlasts any toolA second copy of your PDFs
ClaudeOne bounded task at a time: extract, compare, critique, editA draft you still have to checkDeciding what is true
YouCheck each claim against its source, interpret, decide, writeClaims you can defend
 
research question
  → Zotero: the paper, its PDF, your annotations
  → Obsidian 10_Sources: your annotations, exported (a snapshot you can redo)
  → Claude: one task, answer saved in 60_AI_Drafts (quarantine)
  → you: check each claim in the PDF
  → Obsidian 20_Literature and 30_Synthesis: your notes and comparisons
  → research question, then writing
  → Zotero: inserts the citations

Keep three things apart: storing a paper (Zotero), understanding it (your literature note), and using it to support a claim (an evidence row with a locator and a quote you found yourself).

Model and harness

The model only predicts text. Everything around it is the harness: the instructions it sees, the tools it can call, the loop that lets it act and check, and what it may do without asking. A connector built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), like the Zotero connector, is one part of a harness: it gives Claude tools for an outside system. Each harness changes what you have to check. Unfamiliar terms are in the Glossary.

HarnessWhat it addsNew thing to check
Chat, Claude aloneNothing beyond the model and your promptReferences and facts made up from memory
Research modeMany linked web searches, with citationsSummaries of sources you haven't opened
Claude Code in your vault, with the Zotero connectorReads your notes and your library's full text; writes files; follows rules you setA finding from the wrong paper; a claim stated more strongly than the paper does
Claude Code in a project (Weeks 2–4)Runs code; skills, subagents, hooksCode that runs but is wrong
Claude Slides and uploaded skills (Week 4)Makes decks in the app; skills that you upload guide how it plans a talkPolished slides with wrong numbers

Setup A

Claude, Zotero, and Obsidian

everyone together; help your neighbor

Do the steps in order. Each says what you should see when it worked. Your "Done" ticks are saved in this browser only. Stuck? Raise a hand and move to the next step that doesn't depend on it.

Check your Claude account

What and why

Claude is the AI model; claude.ai, the desktop app, and Claude Code are different ways of using it (different harnesses), all tied to one account. The Pro plan unlocks Research mode, Cowork, and Claude Code. The privacy setting decides whether your chats may train future models, and the capability switches decide which tools Claude may use in chat: web search lets it look things up, code execution lets it run code and make files. Checking them now means every lab starts from the same settings.

  1. At claude.ai, open the account menu: it should say Pro.
  2. Settings → Privacy: the training setting is off.
  3. Settings → Capabilities: turn on Code execution and file creation and web search.

Install the Claude desktop app

What and why

The desktop app is the same Claude as the website, plus features that need your computer: Cowork, which works on folders you choose, and connectors to programs running on your machine. Your chats sync between the app, the browser, and your phone. You'll use it for chat and Research mode in Labs 1–2 and for Cowork in Week 4.

Download it from claude.ai (macOS or Windows), install, open it, and sign in with the same account.

You should see

Your chats from the browser, and Pro in the account menu.

On Linux

There's no desktop app. Use claude.ai in the browser for Labs 1 and 2 and Claude Code (Setup B) for the rest.

Install Zotero and its browser connector

What and why

Zotero is a reference manager: a database of the papers you've collected (authors, title, journal, DOI), with their PDFs, your highlights and comments, and a way to insert formatted citations into a manuscript. The Zotero Connector is a browser extension that saves the paper you're looking at, its details and usually its PDF, into Zotero in one click. Sync keeps a backup of your library on zotero.org. In this course Zotero is your one trusted list of sources: nothing goes in until you've checked that it exists.

  1. From zotero.org/download, install the Zotero desktop app (version 10), then the Zotero Connector for your browser.
  2. Create a free Zotero account at zotero.org. In Zotero, open Settings → Sync and sign in, so your library is backed up online.

You should see

A Zotero icon in your browser toolbar. On a journal article page it changes to a page or paper icon.

Don't

Don't move Zotero's data folder into Dropbox, OneDrive, iCloud, or Google Drive. Zotero warns that this can corrupt its database. Use Zotero's own sync instead.

Add the three class papers

What and why

A collection is a folder-like group inside your library; one item can sit in several collections without being copied. A DOI (Digital Object Identifier) is the permanent ID of a published paper. Adding by DOI pulls the official record from the publisher, so the details are right from the start, and Zotero tries to attach the PDF. Checking the record and the PDF now matters because everything later, your notes and Claude's reading, depends on having the right paper, and the whole paper.

  1. In Zotero, right-click My Library → New Collection. Name it exactly FWE875 – Class corpus.
  2. Select the collection. Click the Add Item by Identifier button (the magic wand) in the toolbar, paste the first DOI below, press Enter. Repeat for the other two.
  3. Click each item and check, in the item pane on the right: title, authors, year, journal, DOI.
  4. Expand each item (the triangle). Is there a PDF? Is it the article itself, not a supplement? Its page count should match the table.
Key we usePaperDOIPagesKind of study
abatzoglou2016Abatzoglou & Williams (2016), Impact of anthropogenic climate change on wildfire across western US forests. PNAS 113(42):11770–1177510.1073/pnas.16071711136Observational: fuel aridity and forest area burned, western US
li2023Li et al. (2023), AttentionFire_v1.0: interpretable machine learning fire model for burned-area predictions over tropics. Geoscientific Model Development 16(3):869–88410.5194/gmd-16-869-202316Machine learning: predicting burned area in Africa and South America
rabin2017Rabin et al. (2017), The Fire Modeling Intercomparison Project (FireMIP), phase 1: experimental and analytical protocols with detailed model descriptions. Geoscientific Model Development 10(3):1175–119710.5194/gmd-10-1175-201723Protocol: how global fire models will be run and compared

The two GMD papers are open access (Creative Commons). The PNAS paper is free to read on the PNAS site. The instructor is one of the authors of Li et al. (2023).

If no PDF was attached

Open the DOI in your browser (https://doi.org/ plus the DOI), download the PDF from the article page, and drag it onto the item in Zotero. It becomes the item's attachment. For the PNAS paper, if the PDF link asks you to sign in, use the UW–Madison Libraries link (on campus, or through the library's off-campus access).

Back up your Zotero library

What and why

Zotero keeps your whole library in one folder, its data directory: a database file (zotero.sqlite) and a storage folder holding the PDFs. Copying that folder while Zotero is closed gives you a restore point. Later today you'll connect an AI agent to this library. Its write tools are blocked in this course, but backing up before any agent can reach your data is the habit worth keeping.

  1. Settings → Advanced → Files and Folders → Show Data Directory. A folder opens.
  2. Quit Zotero.
  3. Copy that whole folder somewhere safe and name it with today's date. Reopen Zotero.

Install Obsidian and open the class vault

What and why

Obsidian is a note-taking app that works on ordinary text files written in Markdown, plain text with light formatting marks such as # for a heading, kept in a folder on your computer. A vault is simply that folder: Obsidian shows its files as notes, lets you link notes to one another, and adds properties, search, and tables, but the notes stay plain files that you own and can read without Obsidian. That's why the course uses it: your thinking isn't locked into an app, and Claude Code can read the same files. The starter vault gives you a structure that encodes the workflow: numbered folders keep what Zotero exports (10_Sources), your own notes (20–50), and Claude's unchecked drafts (60_AI_Drafts) apart, and CLAUDE.md tells Claude Code how to behave in this vault.

  1. Install Obsidian from obsidian.md/download. It's free, including for work use, and you don't need an account.
  2. Download the starter vault:
fwe875-vault.zipEmpty research vault: folders, templates, CLAUDE.md, permission rules, a reading table
  1. Unzip it into a folder you'll keep, for example Documents/fwe875/. You get a folder called FWE875-vault.
  2. Open Obsidian. Next to Open folder as vault, click Open and choose FWE875-vault.
  3. If Obsidian asks about community plugins, leave Restricted mode on. This course uses none.
  4. Open README.md and read it. It explains every folder.
  5. Open Settings → Core plugins and check that Bases is on. Then open Reading-queue.base: an empty table that will list your literature notes.

You should see

In the file list on the left: numbered folders from 00_Inbox to 99_Admin, plus CLAUDE.md, README.md, and Reading-queue.base.

If you can't see the .claude folder

That's normal. Obsidian and your file browser hide folders whose names start with a dot. It holds the rules Claude Code will follow in this vault, and you'll look at them in Setup B.

Lab 1

Your project summary, then the prompt ladder

individualno AI for the summaryClaude app

Goal: write down your project in your own words, then find out, on a task of your own, which parts of a prompt change Claude's answer. The summary is the context you'll give Claude all term, so it has to come from you first.

Write your project summary (no AI)

What and why

A project summary is the short description of your research you'd give a colleague: the question, the system, the data, your progress, and where you're stuck. Every prompt you write this term needs that context, and a model can only use the context you give it. Writing it yourself first makes you state what you know and don't know, and it is what later lets you judge whether Claude's answers fit your project.

  1. In Obsidian, right-click 40_Projects → New note, and name it project-summary.
  2. Write about 150 words, in plain sentences, covering: your question; the system or study area; the data you have or plan to use; what you've done so far; and what you're stuck on.
  3. No project yet? Write it about the class question, or about a question you might study.

Example (a made-up student)

I'm a first-year MS student asking whether spring drought predicts how much area burns in the pine barrens of northwest Wisconsin, where most fires happen in April and May before green-up. My study area is four counties. I plan to use state wildfire records and daily weather from Daymet, with no fieldwork. So far I've plotted fires per year and spring VPD for 2001–2024, and the wettest springs do have few fires. I'm stuck on two things: most fires are small and started by people, so I don't know whether burned area or the number of fires is the better response; and a few big years dominate every plot, so I'm not sure how to model years with almost no fire.

Why no AI here

If Claude writes your summary, every later answer will be built on its guess about your project, not on your project. A rough summary in your words beats a polished one in Claude's.

Set up the ladder

What and why

A prompt ladder is a small experiment on your own prompting: one task, run several times, each time adding one element. Because only one thing changes from rung to rung, you can see which element changed the answer. Claude in the chat app sees only what is in that chat: what you type, paste, or attach. It can't open your vault, so prompt-ladder.md is your lab notebook, not something Claude reads. You write or copy each prompt there, paste it into the chat, and bring the answer back to score it. A new chat for each rung matters for the same reason: within one chat, Claude sees everything said before, so earlier rungs would leak into later ones.

The worksheet is a fill-in template for this lab, in the gray box below: a table where you score each rung, a heading per rung for the exact prompt you send and the start of Claude's answer, and two questions to answer at the end. You keep it as a note in your vault.

  1. Click Copy as Markdown on the worksheet box below.
  2. In Obsidian, right-click 00_Inbox → New note, name it prompt-ladder, and paste (Cmd+V or Ctrl+V). Obsidian shows the table as a table; click in a cell to type.
Prompt-ladder worksheet
Task (one sentence):

| Rung | Element added | Accuracy | Specificity | Usefulness | What changed? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | none | | | | |
| 1 | task and audience | | | | |
| 2 | context (my summary) | | | | |
| 3 | constraints and format | | | | |
| 4 | ask me first (+ optional example) | | | | |

## Rung 0: none
Prompt I sent:

First paragraph of the answer:

## Rung 1: task and audience
Prompt I sent:

First paragraph of the answer:

## Rung 2: context (my summary)
Prompt I sent:

First paragraph of the answer:

## Rung 3: constraints and format
Prompt I sent:

First paragraph of the answer:

## Rung 4: ask me first
Prompt I sent:

First paragraph of the answer:

## What I learned
Biggest jump came from rung __ because ...
What Claude's questions showed was missing from my summary: ...
  1. In the note, after Task (one sentence):, write a task you'd really ask Claude about your project, for example: "Explain to my committee why studies of fire weather use vapor pressure deficit rather than relative humidity."
  2. Open the Claude desktop app (or claude.ai). You'll use a new chat for every rung, so earlier rungs don't leak into later ones.

How each rung works

  1. Write the rung's prompt in the worksheet, under that rung's heading ("Prompt I sent").
  2. Copy it, start a new chat in the Claude app, paste it into the message box, and send.
  3. Copy the first paragraph of Claude's answer into the worksheet ("First paragraph of the answer"), then score it in the table.

Claude never sees the worksheet or anything else in your vault. In this lab it sees only what you paste into the chat. In Lab 4 you'll use Claude Code, which runs in your vault folder and can read its notes when you ask.

Rung 0: the bare prompt

What and why

The bare prompt is your baseline. With no task, audience, or context, the model can only give the most generic answer its training suggests. Every later rung is measured against this one.

  1. New chat. Type only: Tell me about [your topic].
  2. Paste the first paragraph of the answer into the worksheet and score it 1–5 for accuracy, specificity, and usefulness.

You should see

A general overview: accurate-sounding, not about your project, often longer than you need.

Rungs 1–3: add one element at a time

What and why

Each element answers a question the model would otherwise have to guess. Task and audience say what to produce and for whom; context (your summary) says what situation it's for; constraints and format say how long, how structured, and how to flag uncertainty. The [CHECK] markers turn the answer into something you can verify piece by piece instead of something you have to trust whole.

New chat for each. Keep everything from the previous rung and add one thing:

  1. Task and audience: "Write a 200-word explanation of X for my thesis committee, who are ecologists but not atmospheric scientists."
  2. Context: open 40_Projects/project-summary.md, copy the text, and paste it inside <context> … </context> tags above the task. Claude can't open the note itself, so the summary reaches it only by pasting.
  3. Constraints and format: add a <constraints> block: length, structure, "Say when you are unsure," "Mark any claim I should verify with [CHECK]."

Score each one.

You should see

A clear jump at context or constraints. With constraints, [CHECK] markers appear next to specific claims; those are your to-verify list.

Rung 4: let Claude ask you first

What and why

Asking Claude to question you first reverses the usual flow: instead of filling gaps with guesses, it names them. XML tags such as <context> and <task> mark which part of the prompt is which, so your material isn't mistaken for instructions. The questions it asks are a quick check on what your summary left out.

New chat. Paste the full template below with your details, including the last line. The <example> block is optional: use it if you have a passage whose style you want. Answer Claude's questions in one reply, then read and score the result.

Rung 4 template
<context>
[Your project summary]
</context>

<task>
[Exactly what you want, e.g., "Write a 200-word explanation of X"]
The reader is [audience and what they already know].
</task>

<constraints>
- Length: [e.g., under 250 words]
- Structure: [e.g., three short paragraphs]
- Say when you are unsure, and mark any factual claim I should verify with [CHECK].
- Do not invent citations. If a source would help, describe what to search for.
</constraints>

<example>
[Optional: a short passage in the style or structure you want]
</example>

Before you answer, ask me up to 5 questions that would most improve your answer.

You should see

Up to five questions before any answer, such as how technical to be or which fire regime you mean. Notice which ones you hadn't thought about: they often show what your summary left out.

If the example makes things worse

Claude may copy your example's content instead of its style. Say "Use the example for structure and tone only."

Compare, save, log

What and why

Saving the prompt that worked starts your own prompt library, so good prompts are reused and improved rather than reinvented. The AI-use log is your running record of what AI did in your work and how you checked it. It makes your use reproducible and lets you write an honest disclosure later; starting it on day one makes it a habit rather than a chore at the end.

  1. In the worksheet: which rung gave the biggest jump? Did any rung make the answer longer without making it better?
  2. If Claude's questions in rung 4 showed a gap in your summary, fix the summary yourself.
  3. Paste your best prompt into 99_Admin/prompts.md. You'll reuse it.
  4. Open 99_Admin/ai-use-log.csv in Excel or a text editor (Obsidian doesn't open .csv files) and write entry 1: tool (Claude chat), purpose, prompt file, what you kept, and how you'd verify the [CHECK] claims. For what a finished log looks like, open 99_Admin/ai-use-log-example.csv (also on the AI rules and data page).

Lab 2

Citation audit: chat vs. Research mode

break-itclass tally

Goal: measure, on your own topic, how often AI-supplied references are wrong, and how the kind of error changes when Claude can search. In Lab 4 you'll see a third harness: Claude reading papers you chose.

Round 1: plain chat, no tools

What and why

In plain chat with web search off, Claude has no way to look anything up: it writes references from patterns learned in training. That makes this a clean test of how often generated references are wrong, with the model cut off from the tools that usually surround it.

  1. In the desktop app (or claude.ai), open a new chat. In the tools menu of the message box, turn web search off.
  2. Run this prompt with your topic (from your project summary):
Round 1 and 2 prompt
List 8 peer-reviewed journal articles on [your project topic]. For each, give authors, year, title, journal, volume, pages, and DOI.

You should see

Eight neat references with DOIs, and no tool steps in the conversation: nothing was looked up.

Verify every round 1 reference

What and why

A DOI resolves to one specific publication, so a DOI that opens a different paper means the citation is wrong. Crossref is the registry where publishers deposit their records, which makes it the authority for year, journal, volume, and pages. The four categories separate different failures (wrong details, invented papers, real papers that don't fit), because each needs a different check.

For each reference, in this order (stop when you've decided):

  1. DOI: paste it after https://doi.org/ in a new tab. Does it open this paper (same title and authors)?
  2. Title: search the exact title in quotes in Google Scholar. No hit is a strong warning sign.
  3. Crossref: search the title at search.crossref.org to check year, journal, volume, and pages.
  4. Topic: read the abstract. Is it about your topic?

Then choose one category and write what was wrong:

  • A, correct: exists and every detail matches.
  • B, real but wrong: exists, but year, authors, journal, pages, or DOI is wrong (a DOI that opens a different paper counts here).
  • C, fabricated: you can't find it anywhere.
  • D, off-target: real and correct, but not about your topic.

Tricky cases

Right paper, wrong year: B. Real author, plausible title, no such paper: C. A preprint cited as a journal article: B.

Round 2: Research mode

What and why

Research mode is a different harness around the same model: it runs many web searches in sequence, reads the pages it finds, and writes an answer with links to its sources. Being able to look papers up means it invents fewer of them, but it adds a new failure: the claim attached to a citation may not be in that source. That's why you open the source and search for the sentence.

  1. New chat. Turn web search on, then choose Research from the + menu in the message box.
  2. Run the same prompt. Research takes a few minutes; finish verifying round 1 while it runs.
  3. When it finishes, verify 3 of its references the same way.
  4. For one of them, click the citation marker in Claude's answer, open the source, and use Find (Ctrl/Cmd+F) to look for the claim Claude attached to it.

You should see

Many search steps, then an answer with clickable citations. Fewer invented papers. The new question: is the cited sentence really in the source?

Tally and discuss

What and why

Pooling the class's counts turns individual anecdotes into data about this model on these tasks. Comparing the two rounds shows that a better harness changes the kind of error more than it removes the need to check.

  1. Enter your counts in the shared class spreadsheet.
  2. Class discussion: which category was hardest to catch? What changed with search: the number of errors, or the kind?
Worksheet
| # | First author, year (as given) | How I checked | Category (A/B/C/D) | What was wrong |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | | | | |
| 2 | | | | |
| 3 | | | | |
| 4 | | | | |
| 5 | | | | |
| 6 | | | | |
| 7 | | | | |
| 8 | | | | |

Round 1 totals: A __ B __ C __ D __
Round 2 (Research mode, 3 checked): A __ B __ C __ D __ · cited sentence found in source? __

Rule to keep

An AI-supplied reference is a lead, not a citation. Never cite a paper you have not opened.

Setup B

The Zotero connector and Claude Code

terminal

Now you connect Claude to your library and your vault. Everything here happens in a terminal: Terminal on macOS, PowerShell on Windows. You need four commands: cd (change folder), ls or dir (list files), mkdir (make a folder), and claude. Paste commands one line at a time unless a block says otherwise.

Install uv (a tool installer)

What and why

A terminal (Terminal on macOS, PowerShell on Windows) is a text window where you type commands; in this course you copy them in, you don't memorize them. uv installs Python programs, and the Python version each one needs, in its own isolated space, so they don't interfere with each other or with any Python already on your computer. The Zotero connector you install next is a Python program, which is why uv comes first.

You don't need pip for anything in this course.

macOS (Terminal)

curl -LsSf https://astral.sh/uv/install.sh | sh
source $HOME/.local/bin/env
uv --version

Windows (PowerShell)

powershell -ExecutionPolicy ByPass -c "irm https://astral.sh/uv/install.ps1 | iex"
# close PowerShell and open a new one, then:
uv --version

You should see

A version number, like uv 0.x.y.

If "command not found: uv"

Open a new terminal window and try again. With Homebrew on a Mac, brew install uv also works.

Install the Zotero connector

What and why

This connector is an MCP server: a small program that gives Claude a set of tools for an outside system, here "search my Zotero library", "read this item's full text", and "list my annotations". MCP (Model Context Protocol) is the open standard Claude uses to talk to such programs. This one is open-source software from an independent developer, not from Zotero or Anthropic, which is why the course pins a version and limits what it may do.

The course uses the open-source zotero-mcp server. It isn't made by Zotero or Anthropic. If Canvas names a specific version, use that one.

uv tool install zotero-mcp-server
uv tool update-shell        # puts it on your PATH; then open a NEW terminal window
zotero-mcp version

You should see

A version number.

If "zotero-mcp: command not found"

The PATH change applies to new windows only. Close the terminal, open a new one, try again.

Turn on Zotero's local API

What and why

An API is a way for one program to ask another for information. Zotero's local API lets programs on your own computer, such as the connector, ask the running Zotero app for items and files; it can't be reached from other computers. It also lets any program on your computer read your library without a password, so use it with a library that holds nothing sensitive.

In Zotero: Settings → Advanced, under Miscellaneous, tick Allow other applications on this computer to communicate with Zotero.

Install git

What and why

git is version control: it records snapshots, called commits, of a project folder, so you can see exactly what changed and when, and go back. Once an AI agent can edit your files, git is your undo button and your audit trail. Telling git your name and email labels each snapshot with who made it.

macOS: run git --version; if it's missing, macOS offers to install it. Windows: install Git for Windows first; Claude Code on Windows uses its Bash. Then tell git who you are:

git config --global user.name "Your Name"
git config --global user.email "you@wisc.edu"

On a Mac, if the install takes long, carry on and finish it at home: you won't use git until Week 2.

Install Claude Code

What and why

Claude Code is Claude working as an agent in a folder on your computer: it can read and edit the files there, run commands, and use connectors, asking your permission as it goes. Unlike chat, it can act on your files, which is why the course pairs it with written rules and your own checks. claude doctor checks the installation and its settings.

Follow Anthropic's setup page. The recommended installer is one line:

macOS, Linux

curl -fsSL https://claude.ai/install.sh | bash

Windows PowerShell

irm https://claude.ai/install.ps1 | iex

Open a new terminal window, then check:

claude --version
claude doctor

You should see

A version number, then a health report with no errors.

If "claude: command not found"

Open a new terminal window. Still failing? Follow "Fix your PATH" on Anthropic's install troubleshooting page.

Connect Zotero to Claude Code

What and why

This command registers the Zotero MCP server with Claude Code. From then on, each time Claude Code starts it launches the connector in the background and gains its tools. Registering it once for your user account makes it available in every folder you open, and ZOTERO_LOCAL=true tells it to read the library on this computer rather than going through zotero.org with a key.

Run this in a normal terminal, not inside a Claude Code session. Copy the whole line:

claude mcp add --scope user --env ZOTERO_LOCAL=true --transport stdio zotero -- zotero-mcp

What it means: --scope user = available in every folder you open with Claude Code; --env ZOTERO_LOCAL=true = read the Zotero library on this computer, no API key; --transport stdio = run it as a local program; zotero = the name Claude Code shows; everything after -- = the program to run. Keep --transport stdio between --env and the name: without it, Claude Code reads zotero as a second setting and rejects the command.

claude mcp list

You should see

A line like zotero: zotero-mcp - ✓ Connected.

If it says "Failed to connect"

  • Is Zotero open?
  • Claude Code may not find the program. Get its full path and add the server again with that path: 
    which zotero-mcp
# e.g. /Users/you/.local/bin/zotero-mcp
claude mcp remove zotero
claude mcp add --scope user --env ZOTERO_LOCAL=true --transport stdio zotero -- /Users/you/.local/bin/zotero-mcp
    (Get-Command zotero-mcp).Source
# e.g. C:\Users\you\.local\bin\zotero-mcp.exe
claude mcp remove zotero
claude mcp add --scope user --env ZOTERO_LOCAL=true --transport stdio zotero -- C:\Users\you\.local\bin\zotero-mcp.exe
  • claude mcp get zotero shows exactly what Claude Code will run.

First session, in your vault

What and why

Claude Code works in the folder you start it from, and at the start of each session it reads that folder's CLAUDE.md and .claude/settings.json. Starting it inside the vault means it follows the vault's rules: what it may edit, and which tools are blocked. Trusting the folder confirms you meant to let an agent work there, and each permission prompt is a control point where you see an action before it happens.

macOS

cd ~/Documents/fwe875/FWE875-vault
claude

Windows PowerShell

cd $HOME\Documents\fwe875\FWE875-vault
claude

If cd says the folder doesn't exist (common when Windows keeps Documents in OneDrive), type cd and a space, drag the FWE875-vault folder from Finder or File Explorer into the terminal window, and press Enter.

  1. The first time, a browser opens to sign in. Use your Pro account.
  2. Claude Code asks whether you trust this folder. Say yes.
  3. Type /mcp: zotero should be listed as connected. Press Esc.
  4. Type /permissions and look at the deny list. These rules come from the vault's .claude/settings.json: Claude may not edit your own notes, may not search the web here, and may not use any Zotero tool that changes your library. Press Esc.
  5. Ask: "How many items are in my Zotero collection FWE875 – Class corpus? List their titles and whether each has a PDF."
  6. Claude asks permission to use a Zotero tool. Read what it wants to do, then allow it.

You should see

A permission prompt naming a Zotero tool, then three titles, each with a PDF. Leave Claude Code open for Lab 4, or type /exit.

Why Claude asks before acting here

Recent versions of Claude Code start in auto mode, where a second model approves actions instead of you. The vault's settings start it in Manual mode ("defaultMode": "default") so you see every action while you learn what it does. Shift+Tab cycles through the modes; the current one is shown under the prompt.

Optional, later: connect Zotero to the Claude desktop app as well

Run zotero-mcp setup in a terminal. It adds the connector to the desktop app and offers semantic search (finding papers by meaning). Suggested answers:

QuestionAnswerWhy
Configure semantic search?y, or n if the install gives you troubleNothing in class depends on it
Embedding model1 (default, local)Runs on your laptop: no API key, and your papers stay on it
Database update2 (auto, on startup)Pick 3 (daily) if Claude starts slowly
PDF max pages30Covers whole journal articles
Parallel extraction workersAbout half your cores; 1 if unsureFaster first indexing
Zotero database pathPress EnterType a path only if you moved Zotero's data folder

If semantic search later complains about missing packages: uv tool install --force "zotero-mcp-server[semantic]". A semantic-search hit is a candidate to read, not proof that Claude read the paper or searched the whole literature.

Writing to Zotero (adding notes or tags) needs extra authorization (zotero-mcp authorize-local on Zotero 10). This course doesn't use it: AI output goes to your vault's quarantine folder instead.

Other problems

The item count doesn't match. Ask Claude to list the titles and compare. Items in the trash, child notes, and attachments can be counted differently. Say which you mean.

My university laptop blocks installs. Ask your department's IT to install Zotero, uv, and Claude Code, or use a personal laptop. Until then, pair with a neighbor.

On Windows, Claude Code can't run Bash. Install Git for Windows, or let it use PowerShell.

Lab 3

Read, annotate, export to Obsidian

no AItriads: one paper each

Goal: read one paper with a question in mind, mark the evidence in Zotero, and move it into your vault so it stays linked to the exact page. No AI in this lab: you need your own reading before you can judge Claude's.

Form groups of three. Count off: person 1 takes abatzoglou2016, person 2 li2023, person 3 rabin2017. In Lab 4 each of you checks Claude's work on the paper you read.

Say why you're reading

What and why

Reading with a stated purpose changes what you notice. Writing that purpose down before you read also gives you something to check afterwards: did the paper answer what you hoped, or something else?

In Obsidian, open 40_Projects/class-question.md. Under "Why I am reading each paper", complete one line for your paper: "I'm reading this because it may tell me ___."

Read and annotate your paper

What and why

An annotation is a highlight in the PDF plus your comment, stored in Zotero and tied to its exact place on the page. The labels separate what the paper reports from how it was done, where its limits are, and what you think, which is the distinction that most often gets lost when you, or an AI, summarize a paper later.

  1. In Zotero, double-click your paper's PDF to open the reader.
  2. Read with the class question in mind. You won't finish the paper, and you don't need to. Where to look:
PaperReadAsk yourself
abatzoglou2016Abstract; the results on fuel aridity and burned area; Methods on the fire data and the fuel-aridity metrics; the paragraphs on what the attribution assumesWhich aridity measure, which response (and on what scale), which ecosystems?
li2023Abstract; Sect. 2 (data and model); Sect. 3.1–3.2 (performance and drivers); Sect. 3.5 (limitations)Is a number about observations or about the model's predictions? Which regions?
rabin2017Abstract; Sect. 2 (the experiments and their forcing data); Sect. 4 (planned analyses); Sect. 5What kind of paper is this? What does it report, and what does it only plan?
  1. Make 4 to 8 annotations. Highlight the passage, then click the highlight and type a comment that starts with one of these labels:
LabelUse it forAsk yourself
[RESULT]Something the paper reportsWhat was measured, where, over what period, compared with what?
[METHOD]Data, design, model, validation, assumptionsHow was this result produced? At what scale?
[LIMIT]A limitation or boundaryDid the authors say it, or is it your concern? Say which.
[IDEA]Your interpretation or next questionNever the paper's conclusion
  1. If time remains, skim the abstracts of the other two papers.

What a useful annotation looks like (made-up paper)

Highlight: "Summer VPD explained 64% of the variance in burned area." Comment: [RESULT] R² vs LOG burned area; Jun–Aug mean VPD; conifer forests only; 1990–2015; regional totals, not single fires. The comment records the measure, the scale, the ecosystem, and the period, which are the things that get lost later.

Why text labels, not just colors

Colors don't survive every export, and nobody remembers what purple meant six months later. A label in the comment travels with the annotation.

Export your annotations into the vault

What and why

Zotero can collect your annotations into a note and export that note as a Markdown file. Saving it in 10_Sources brings your highlights into the vault as plain text, each with a link back to its page, where both Obsidian and Claude Code can read it. The export is a copy; the original stays in Zotero.

  1. Close the reader tab. In the items list, right-click your paper and choose Add Note from Annotations. A child note appears under the item.
  2. Right-click that note and choose Export Note…. Pick Markdown as the format and keep Include Zotero Links ticked.
  3. Save it in your vault's 10_Sources folder, named with your paper's key: abatzoglou2016.md, li2023.md, or rabin2017.md.

You should see

The file in Obsidian under 10_Sources: each highlight as a quoted paragraph, followed by a citation, a small pdf link, and your comment.

A snapshot, not a live link

This file is a copy. When you change annotations in Zotero, export again and replace it. That's why your own thinking never goes in 10_Sources.

Test a link back to the page

What and why

zotero:// links are addresses that open a particular item, page, or annotation inside the Zotero app, the way https:// links open pages in a browser. They keep every note one click away from the evidence it quotes.

In Obsidian, switch the note to reading view (the book icon at top right) and click one pdf link.

You should see

Zotero comes to the front with the PDF open at that annotation.

If nothing happens

Your computer doesn't know that zotero:// links belong to Zotero. Reopen Zotero and try again. On Linux this may not work at all; use the page number in the citation instead.

Start your literature note

What and why

A literature note is your own account of a paper: what it says, the evidence, its limits, and what you make of it, in your words. Properties are labelled fields at the top of the note (citekey, source_coverage, ai_status) that Obsidian can sort and filter; the reading table is built from them. The evidence ledger is a table of claims, each with a locator and a short exact quote, so every claim stays checkable.

  1. In Obsidian, right-click 90_Templates/literature-note.md → Make a copy. Drag the copy into 20_Literature and rename it with your paper's key.
  2. In the properties at the top, set citekey to the key. Set source_coverage to what you actually read: probably selected_sections, not full_text.
  3. Replace REPLACE_WITH_KEY in the Source export link, so it opens your file in 10_Sources.
  4. Write your one-sentence takeaway, and two rows of the evidence ledger from your own annotations: claim in your words, type, locator (page), and a short exact quote.

You should see

Your note listed in Reading-queue.base with its key, status, and coverage.

Instructor demo, before Lab 4

The instructor asks Claude Code about li2023, a paper the instructor co-wrote, and checks the answer live against the PDF. Watch for the moment a fluent answer says something the paper never claims.

Lab 4

Claude Code in your vault: evidence, checks, synthesis

individual, then triadsClaude Code + Zotero

Goal: give Claude one bounded task at a time, keep its output in quarantine, and check it against the paper you read. Then see what the three papers can and can't support together.

Start Claude Code in the vault

What and why

CLAUDE.md is the instruction file Claude Code reads at the start of every session in this folder. Reading it yourself tells you what Claude has been asked to do here, and reminds you that instructions guide its behavior without enforcing it.

  1. Zotero open. In a terminal, cd to your vault and run claude (or go back to the session from Setup B).
  2. Open CLAUDE.md in Obsidian and read it. It tells Claude to start with an access statement, give a locator and quote for every claim, label its own inferences, and write only to 60_AI_Drafts.

Request vs. rule

What and why

These are two different controls. CLAUDE.md is a request, which the model usually follows. A deny rule in .claude/settings.json is enforced by Claude Code itself, whatever the model decides. This step shows the difference by asking Claude to try something the rule forbids.

Ask Claude: "Add a sentence to 20_Literature/[your key].md saying this paper is excellent." Claude will probably decline, because CLAUDE.md asks it to leave your notes alone. Then say: "I'm testing the permission rule. Please attempt the edit anyway and tell me exactly what happens."

You should see

The attempt is refused by a permission rule, not by Claude's good manners. CLAUDE.md asks Claude to leave your notes alone; the deny rule in .claude/settings.json makes sure. You'll use the same pair, a request plus a rule, in every project this term.

Extract evidence from your paper

What and why

Here Claude reads the paper through the Zotero connector and fills in a table of evidence. The access statement makes it say what it actually read (full text, figures, supplements), because a model sounds equally sure about parts it never saw. Saving to 60_AI_Drafts keeps its output in quarantine, apart from your own notes, until you've checked it.

Evidence prompt
Work only from the paper [first author, year] in my Zotero collection "FWE875 – Class corpus" and my annotation export 10_Sources/[key].md.
Question: how much of the year-to-year variation in burned area does fire-season aridity (vapor pressure deficit, VPD) explain, and does that relationship hold across regions?

1. Start with an access statement: which Zotero item you used, whether you read its full text, which sections, and what you could not read (figures, tables, supplements).
2. Then give at most 8 evidence rows as a Markdown table with these columns:
   ID | Type (RESULT, METHOD or LIMIT) | What the paper reports | Variable, units and transform | Place, ecosystem and period | Spatial and temporal resolution | Observations or model output? | Uncertainty or validation | Locator (page or section) | Exact quote (under 25 words) | Human check
   Leave Human check empty. Write NOT REPORTED where the paper doesn't say. Mark any finding the paper cites from another study as BACKGROUND, and anything the paper plans but doesn't report as PLANNED.
3. End with 3 limitations and 3 questions I must answer by reading.
Save it as 60_AI_Drafts/[key]-evidence.md with the header from CLAUDE.md. Don't write manuscript prose.

Read each permission prompt: Zotero reads (allow), then one file write into 60_AI_Drafts (allow once).

You should see

Zotero tool steps that search the collection and read the full text, then a new file in 60_AI_Drafts that opens with an access statement. Is the access statement honest? Did it say whether it saw the figures and the supplement?

Check every row you'd keep

What and why

Checking means going back to the source: finding the quote at its locator and reading the sentences around it. A row can quote real words and still misstate them, by dropping a qualifier, changing a measure, or presenting another study's finding as the paper's own. Only rows you've checked move into your notes.

  1. Open the evidence file in Obsidian, next to the PDF in Zotero.
  2. For each row: go to the locator, use Find (Ctrl/Cmd+F) with 3–4 words of the quote, and read the sentences around it.
  3. In the Human check column, write ✓ (the quote is there and the row keeps its scope and units), partly (the quote exists but the row overstates it or loses a qualifier), or ✗ (not there, or says something else). Add a few words on why.
  4. Copy the rows you marked ✓ into the evidence ledger of your own note in 20_Literature. Fix any wording first. In the "How I checked" column, write "found on p. X". Set that note's ai_status to used-verified.

What to check in every row

The measure and its scale (raw, log, mean, maximum); which variables went into it; the place, ecosystem, and period; whether a number comes from observations or from a model; whether the paper reports it or only plans it; and whether it is the paper's own finding or one it cites from another study. Checking that the quote exists isn't enough: does it support the row, at the same scope?

Compare the three papers

What and why

A comparison matrix lines studies up on the same rows (measure, place, scale, method, uncertainty), which shows what can and can't be combined. AI drafts one quickly; its value comes from each triad member checking the column for the paper they read.

Matrix prompt
Use only the three papers in my Zotero collection "FWE875 – Class corpus" and the files in 10_Sources/ and 60_AI_Drafts/.
Question: what must be resolved before these three papers can be used together to say how much fire-season aridity explains year-to-year burned area, and whether that holds across regions?

Make a matrix with one column per paper and these rows: kind of study (observational analysis, machine-learning prediction, model protocol…); aridity or weather variables and the months they cover; response variable, units and transform; place, ecosystems and period; spatial and temporal resolution; how "explained" is measured (R², error, variable importance) and whether it is computed on observations or on model output; validation and uncertainty; what the study was designed to answer.
Give a locator for every cell. Mark cells that can't be compared across papers.
Below the matrix, list separately: agreement; real contradictions; differences of scope; missing information; your own proposals, labelled [SUGGESTION].
Don't conclude that something is unstudied in general because these three papers don't cover it.
Save as 60_AI_Drafts/matrix.md.

In your triad, each person checks the column for the paper they read and marks any cell that's wrong or overstated.

Diagnose a planted claim

What and why

A planted claim is a sentence built to sound supported by real citations while overreaching each one. Splitting it into separate claims and checking each against its source is the core skill of reviewing. Comparing your triad's audit with Claude's shows where an AI check adds something and where it shares your blind spots.

This sentence was written to sound right. It is not:

Planted claim (not a finding)

"Fire-season vapor pressure deficit alone explains about 76% of year-to-year burned area in western US forests (Abatzoglou & Williams, 2016) and 66–80% across tropical Africa and South America (Li et al., 2023), and the eleven FireMIP models reproduce this aridity–burned-area relationship globally (Rabin et al., 2017), showing that it holds across regions."

  1. Triad, no AI: split the sentence into its separate claims. For each one, the person who read the cited paper says whether the paper supports it, and what it actually shows.
  2. Then ask Claude:
Audit prompt
Audit this sentence against the three papers in "FWE875 – Class corpus". Do not rewrite it.
"[paste the planted claim]"
Split it into its separate claims. For each, say: supported, partly supported, unsupported, contradicted, or not assessable from these papers, with the paper and locator that justify the call.
Flag changed measures, transforms or units, missing qualifiers, numbers computed on model output presented as observations, results that a paper only plans, causal language, transfer to a region or ecosystem the paper didn't study, and any finding that is background the paper cites from another study.
Show me where I must check your assessment myself. Save it as 60_AI_Drafts/planted-claim-audit.md.
  1. Compare: what did your triad catch that Claude didn't, and the reverse?

A second AI pass isn't an independent check

Claude auditing Claude can share the same blind spots. The independent check is you, with the paper open.

Lab 5

From gap to research question

individualclass project, or your own

Goal: use AI first to widen your options, then to attack your favorite. Work on the class project by default. If your own project's literature is ready, use it instead. Checking whether your question has already been answered (the novelty scan) is a suggested practice after class.

Your gap statement first

What and why

A research gap is a specific combination of question, place, scale, measure, or method that existing work doesn't cover. Writing yours before asking AI keeps the starting point yours and gives you something to compare Claude's suggestions against.

Without AI, in 40_Projects/class-question.md: three sentences on what these papers leave open. Be specific: which combination of question, place, scale, measure, or method is missing?

Weak vs. strong

Weak: "More research is needed on fire and climate."
Strong: "The three papers mean different things by 'explained': R² of log burned area against a mix of aridity measures in one region's forests, R² of a machine-learning model's own predictions in Africa and South America, and, in the protocol paper, nothing yet. None compares the year-to-year relationship within regions across regions whose fires are limited by fuel rather than by dryness."

Diverge: your questions, then Claude's

What and why

Diverging means generating many options before judging any. AI helps here because it can vary scale, mechanism, measure, and method quickly; asking it not to rank them keeps the choice with you.

  1. Write 3 research questions yourself first.
  2. Then, in Claude Code, run the brainstorm prompt.
Brainstorm prompt
Read 40_Projects/class-question.md (the question and my gap statement) and 60_AI_Drafts/matrix.md.
Propose 10 research questions that address the gaps. Make them differ on purpose: vary the spatial or temporal scale, the mechanism, the measure of aridity or of fire, and the method. Assume the study can use only public data, computation, and a small class dataset (burned area and fire-season VPD for ten regions over twenty years): no new fieldwork.
For each, give one line for the question and one line for the data it would need.
Don't rank them, and don't tell me which is best. Save as 60_AI_Drafts/rq-brainstorm.md.

You should see

Ten questions that differ in scale, mechanism, measure, and method, each with its data, and no ranking.

Converge: score and pick one

What and why

FINER (feasible, interesting, novel, ethical, relevant) is a standard checklist for judging research questions. Scoring them yourself, before any AI critique, records your own judgment. A hypothesis with a direction states the result you expect, so the data have a chance to contradict it.

Choose your top 4 from all 13. Score each yourself: Feasible, Interesting, Novel, Ethical, Relevant (S strong, A adequate, W weak). Pick one and write a hypothesis with a direction ("drier fire seasons → more area burned, more strongly in forests than in grasslands").

AI critique

What and why

Asking Claude to act as a skeptical committee member is role prompting: it steers the model away from its tendency to agree and praise. Treat its points as a list to weigh, accepting or rejecting each with a reason, because the question stays yours.

Critic prompt
Act as a skeptical member of my thesis committee in [your field]. Do not praise the question.

<project>[the class question, or your project summary from 40_Projects/project-summary.md]</project>
<question>[your research question]</question>
<hypothesis>[your hypothesis]</hypothesis>

1. Rate the question on each FINER criterion (feasible, interesting, novel, ethical, relevant) as strong, adequate, or weak, with one sentence of reasoning each.
2. Can this question be answered with public data and computation? If not, what is missing?
3. What result would falsify the hypothesis?
4. List three alternative explanations for the pattern I expect.
5. Propose three reframings: one narrower, one broader, and one testing a different mechanism.
6. What is the single biggest risk that this project fails?

Mark each point accept or reject, with a reason, in your project note.

Write version 2 yourself

What and why

Revising in your own words, and noting where the decisive change came from, keeps a record of how the question developed and who contributed what. That record is exactly what an honest disclosure needs later.

In 40_Projects/class-question.md, write version 2 of your question and hypothesis, and one line on the change that mattered most and where it came from (you or the critique). You can test it against published work with the novelty scan after class.

Example (class project)

v1: Does VPD control burned area?
v2: Across ten fire regions, 2001–2020, how much of the year-to-year variation in log burned area within each region is explained by fire-season VPD, and is the relationship weaker in dry, fuel-limited regions than in forests?

Exit ticket

Answer on Canvas before you leave. It isn't scored; it tells the instructor what to fix and follow up on:

  1. Which rung of the prompt ladder changed Claude's answer most, and what did Claude's questions show was missing from your summary?
  2. Your Lab 2 round 1 totals (A/B/C/D), and what changed in Research mode.
  3. In Lab 4, how many of Claude's evidence rows for your paper did you mark ✓, partly, and ✗? What was the most common problem?
  4. Which part of the planted claim was hardest to see through, and why?
  5. On a scale of 1 to 5, how much do you trust AI output for your research today? Did it change during the session?
  6. Anything that isn't working on your laptop yet.

Before Week 2

Needed: anything from Setup A or B that isn't finished must work by Week 2 (claude mcp list shows Zotero connected; git --version answers). Week 2 starts with a worked example and then runs on your own data, in the Code tab of the Claude desktop app: bring the data, a draft data dictionary, a one-page plan draft, a GitHub account with GitHub CLI signed in, and the Code tab opening a local session. The full list is under Week 2 → Before class. Suggested: the Week 1 practice, the same workflow on your own project plus the novelty scan, and the readings for Week 2.

← Course homeWeek 2 →

Week 2 · Research stage: experiments and analysis

From a worked example to your own analysis

This week you learn one way of working by doing it twice. First, everyone builds the same small model of a forest's carbon exchange and calibrates and validates it against flux tower data, step by step. Then you apply the same steps to your own project. You work in the Code tab of the Claude desktop app: Claude Code with a window, on a project folder on your computer. Claude Code writes the code; tests, skills, and reviewer agents check it; you decide what is right; git records every version.

In this session

  • Concepts for this week
  • Part A, the worked example: Lab 1: Set up the example · Lab 2: Plan, audit, tests · Lab 3: Build, calibrate, validate · Lab 4: Review and improve
  • Part B, your project: Lab 5: Start your own project
  • Guidelines for any project · Exit ticket

Before class

The first half of the session is a worked example that everyone does with the same data, which you download in class. The second half runs on your own project. Needed:

Windows: if Rscript isn't found

The R installer doesn't add R to the PATH, the list of folders where the terminal looks for programs. Claude Code needs Rscript too. In PowerShell, add the newest installed R to your PATH, then open a new terminal window (and restart the Claude app) and try Rscript --version again:

$rbin = (Get-ChildItem "C:\Program Files\R" -Directory | Sort-Object Name | Select-Object -Last 1).FullName + "\bin"
[Environment]::SetEnvironmentVariable("Path", [Environment]::GetEnvironmentVariable("Path", "User") + ";$rbin", "User")

No data of your own yet? Use the class fire dataset and the class question; you download it in Lab 5.

Suggested: the Week 1 practice, and the readings for this week on the Readings page.

Concepts for this week

The worked example and your own project follow the same loop. Planning happens once; the rest repeats until your definition of done is met.

  1. Planquestion, model, figure, checks; written before results
  2. BuildClaude Code writes code and tests; you approve each step
  3. Reviewtests, skills, three reviewer agents, and you
  4. Decideaccept or reject each finding, with a reason
  5. Fixon a git branch, one finding per commit
  6. Merge and taga named version; then review again

The last four steps repeat. Stop when your definition of done is met and a round finds nothing new you'd call "must fix".

Models and data

The worked example is a small model of a forest's carbon exchange, tested against measurements. These ideas apply to any model you fit to data.

TermWhat it meansWhere
Flux towerAn eddy covariance tower measures the CO2 exchanged between an ecosystem and the air, every half-hour, for years. FLUXNET collects and processes these records from sites worldwide.Lab 1
NEE, GPP, RECONet ecosystem exchange is what the tower measures: respiration (RECO) minus photosynthesis (gross primary production, GPP). GPP and RECO are not measured; they are estimated from NEE with a model ("partitioning").Labs 1–4
CalibrationChoosing a model's parameter values so its output matches observations, by minimizing an objective function such as the sum of squared errors.Lab 3
ValidationTesting the calibrated model on data it has not seen. With time series, hold out whole years: neighbouring days are too alike for a random split to be a fair test.Lab 3
RMSE, bias, NSETypical error, average over- or under-prediction, and Nash–Sutcliffe efficiency (1 is perfect; 0 is no better than the mean of the observations).Lab 3
EquifinalityDifferent parameter sets fit the data about equally well, often by errors that cancel. A parameter stuck at its bound is a common warning sign.Lab 4
Parameter recoveryA known-answer check for calibration code: simulate data with parameters you chose, calibrate, and check you get them back.Lab 2

Claude Code, git, and reproducible projects

The words you need this week, with where you first use each. The Glossary has more.

TermWhat it meansWhere
Claude Code in a window
Code tabClaude Code inside the Claude desktop app. A local session works in one folder on your computer and reads that folder's settings, CLAUDE.md, and skills, the same as claude in a terminal.Labs 1, 5
Permission modeManual asks before every edit and command; Plan explores and proposes, changing nothing; Auto lets a second model approve actions instead of you. The course uses Manual and Plan.Labs 1, 3
Deny ruleA rule in .claude/settings.json that Claude's own edit tools can't break, such as "never edit data/raw/". It doesn't cover scripts or commands Claude runs, so you still read those.Lab 1
Context and usageWhat Claude can hold at once, and your plan's limits. The usage ring next to the model picker shows both. Subagents, plugins, and long files spend them.All labs
Git and GitHub
Commit · tagA commit is a saved snapshot with a message. A tag gives one commit a permanent name, such as v1.Labs 1, 3
Branch · diff · mergeA branch is a separate line of commits for trying fixes. A diff shows exactly what changed. Merging brings a branch's commits into main.Lab 4
GitHub · cloneGitHub keeps a copy of your history online (a remote); private means only you and people you invite can see it. Cloning makes a fresh copy from it.Labs 1, 5; practice
Reproducible projects
ProvenanceWhere each raw file came from, under what terms, and a checksum that proves it is the same file.Labs 1, 5
Lockfilerenv.lock or uv.lock: the exact package versions, committed with the code.Labs 1, 5
Known-answer checkA test where you know the right answer: simulated data with an effect you chose, a value calculated by hand, or a published number from the same data. If the code can't recover a known answer, its answer on your data means little.Labs 2, 5
Clean rerunA fresh clone, the README followed exactly, the same results. Your own computer hides missing steps; a clean clone doesn't.Practice

Five ways to extend Claude

They are easy to mix up. All five are plain files or settings you can read before you use them.

WhatWhat it isWhen it actsIn this course
SkillA folder with a SKILL.md: a name, a description of when to use it, instructions, and sometimes scriptsWhen your request matches its description, or when you pick it from the / menu. Only the name and description sit in context until thenKit: data-audit, review-round, repro-check (Labs 2, 4); published research skills (Lab 5)
SubagentA separate helper with its own context window, instructions, and tool listWhen the main session hands it a task; it reports back and you decideKit: three read-only reviewers (Lab 4)
HookA command that runs automatically at a set point, whatever the model decidesOn an event, such as before a file editWeek 3
Connector (MCP)A link to an outside system's tools or dataWhen Claude calls one of its toolsZotero (Week 1)
PluginA bundle of the above, installed from a marketplace for all your projects on this computer (user scope), one project, or locallyWhenever its parts do; every enabled plugin adds to every message's contextLab 5 (optional)

Skills for procedures, agents for judgment

A skill is a procedure Claude follows in your session, the same way every time, and it can run code. A subagent is a separate reviewer: it starts from the files, not from your conversation, and its tools can read but not edit. Use skills for checklists; use agents for an independent second opinion.

Agents review; you decide

Reviewers find a lot, miss some things, and are sometimes wrong. Every finding gets your decision and your reason in review_log.md. Skills written by others get the same treatment: you read them before you use them.

Your kit

All of these are plain text files in your project's .claude/ folder. You can read them, change them, and share them.

NameKindWhat it doesWhen you use it
data-auditSkillRuns code that checks your raw data against your data dictionary; writes data_audit.md; changes nothingLabs 2 and 5, and whenever data change
review-roundSkill (you start it with /review-round)Runs the tests and the whole pipeline, asks the three reviewers for independent reviews, and adds their findings to review_log.mdLab 4, once per round
repro-checkSkillClones the project into a temporary folder, follows README.md exactly, reruns everything, and compares outputsPractice, and before every tag you share
code-reviewerSubagent, read-onlyCode against the plan: silent data loss, filters and joins, missing codes, units, paths, untested logicCalled by review-round, or by you
stats-reviewerSubagent, read-onlyModel against the plan and the data structure: unit of replication, assumptions, uncertainty, claimsCalled by review-round, or by you
figure-reviewerSubagent, read-onlyOpens the figure and checks it against the code and outputs: hidden data, axes, color, caption numbers, claimsCalled by review-round, or by you

Part A · The worked example

The example in brief

CH-Dav is a Norway spruce forest at 1639 m near Davos, Switzerland, where an eddy covariance tower has measured the forest's CO2 exchange since 1997. You build a four-parameter daily model: photosynthesis (GPP) from light, temperature, and air dryness, and respiration (RECO) from temperature. You calibrate it on 2006–2010, validate it on 2011–2014, have it reviewed, and improve it. The plan, the data, and a data dictionary come with the example, so you can focus on the method. You do the same for your own project in Part B.

Lab 1

Set up the example in the Code tab

individualCode tabgit and GitHub

Goal: a project folder that keeps its own record: the data and where they came from, rules the agent can't break, the package versions you use, and a private copy on GitHub. You check that Claude Code is following the project's rules before you ask it to do anything. Commands follow the language switch in the sidebar.

fwe875-week2-example.zipThe worked example: CH-Dav flux data (FLUXNET2015, CC BY 4.0), data record, plan, CLAUDE.md, skills, and reviewer agents

Make the example project

What and why

The download is a project skeleton with the example's data and documents already in place. data/raw/ holds data exactly as received; data/processed/ and outputs/ will hold only what scripts make, so every result can be rebuilt from the original file. You'll use the same layout for your own project in Part B, so every skill and agent finds things in the same place.

On a Mac, Safari unzips the download for you; with another browser, double-click the zip in Downloads. On Windows, the first command below unzips it. Then, in a terminal:

macOS

mkdir -p ~/Documents/fwe875/carbon-example
cp -R ~/Downloads/carbon-example/. ~/Documents/fwe875/carbon-example/
cd ~/Documents/fwe875/carbon-example
ls -a

Windows PowerShell

Expand-Archive $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-week2-example.zip -DestinationPath $HOME\Downloads -Force
mkdir $HOME\Documents\fwe875\carbon-example
Copy-Item -Recurse -Force $HOME\Downloads\carbon-example\* $HOME\Documents\fwe875\carbon-example
cd $HOME\Documents\fwe875\carbon-example
Get-ChildItem -Force

Then delete the files for the language you don't use:

macOS

rm -r py run_all.py pyproject.toml

Windows PowerShell

Remove-Item -Recurse py, run_all.py, pyproject.toml

macOS

rm -r R run_all.R

Windows PowerShell

Remove-Item -Recurse R, run_all.R

Last, open CLAUDE.md in a text editor: on the Language: line under Rules, keep R or Python, and under "How to run" delete the lines for the other language.

You should see

.claude, .gitignore, CLAUDE.md, README.md, analysis_plan.md, done.md, review_log.md, deviations.md, run_all, and the folders data, outputs, tests, and R or py.

If the copy finds nothing, or makes a folder inside a folder

If carbon-example holds another carbon-example folder instead of the files, the zip was unzipped twice (Windows "Extract All" does this). Delete the folder and Downloads\carbon-example, then run the commands again. Documents in OneDrive: use $HOME\OneDrive\Documents instead of $HOME\Documents, here and in every later step.

Check the data against its record

What and why

Provenance is the record of where data came from, under what terms, and in exactly what version. A SHA-256 checksum is a fingerprint of a file: change one byte and it changes, so anyone can confirm they have the identical file. The flux data here are FLUXNET2015 data under a CC BY 4.0 license: you may share and adapt them, as long as you cite the dataset and the site.

macOS

shasum -a 256 data/raw/*

Windows PowerShell

Get-FileHash data\raw\* -Algorithm SHA256
  1. Compare the result with the SHA-256 in data/README.md. (Windows prints it in capitals; that's the same value.)
  2. Read data/README.md and the data dictionary in CLAUDE.md. In a note, answer: Where did the file come from, and what must you cite? Which column is the measurement, and which columns are estimates made from it? What are the units of VPD_F?

Open the project in the Code tab, and check its rules

What and why

The Code tab runs the same Claude Code you used in a terminal in Week 1, with a window around it: a mode selector, a Terminal pane, and a view of every change. A local session works in the folder you choose and reads that folder's .claude/settings.json, CLAUDE.md, and skills. The project's settings add two rules: start in Manual mode, where Claude asks before every edit and command, and a deny rule that its edit tools can never change data/raw/. Checking that the rules are active, rather than assuming it, is the habit this course asks for with every tool.

  1. In the Claude desktop app, open the Code tab and click + New session (Cmd+N or Ctrl+N). Set the environment to Local and choose the carbon-example folder.
  2. Mode: the mode selector should say Manual. If it says anything else, pick Manual: the app remembers your pick for this folder.
  3. Skills: type / in the prompt box. data-audit, review-round, and repro-check should be in the list. Press Esc.
  4. Deny rule: send the prompt below. Claude should report that the write was refused by your settings. If a permission prompt appears instead, choose No: the rule isn't loaded (see the fix below).
Deny-rule test
Use your file-writing tool, not a shell command, to create a new file data/raw/deny_test.txt containing the word test. Tell me exactly what happened.
  1. Open the Terminal pane: click Terminal in the session's title bar, or press Ctrl+` (on a Mac too). It opens in carbon-example. You'll type this week's commands there. On Windows, the commands on this page are for PowerShell: if the pane opens a different shell, type powershell first.

You should see

The write is refused without a permission prompt, and no deny_test.txt appears in data/raw/. The usage ring next to the model picker shows how much of the context window and of your plan you've used.

If the skills, the mode, or the deny rule don't show

Check you chose carbon-example itself, not fwe875 or Downloads: the session reads .claude/ only from its own folder. Check the hidden .claude folder was copied (ls -a or Get-ChildItem -Force in the Terminal pane). Then start a new session. Some terminal commands, such as /permissions, aren't available in the Code tab; that's expected.

Record your software versions

What and why

A lockfile records the exact version of every package the project uses, so someone else (or you, next year) can install the same versions and get the same results. R's renv writes renv.lock and gives the project its own package library; Python's uv writes uv.lock and a project environment in .venv. Both files are committed, so the versions travel with the code.

In the Terminal pane (it takes a few minutes if packages download):

Rscript -e "renv::init()"

renv finds the packages listed in R/packages.R (testthat and ggplot2), copies them into the project's own library, and writes renv.lock. The model's calibration uses base R's optim(), so nothing else is needed.

uv add pandas numpy scipy matplotlib pytest
uv run python -c "import scipy; print(scipy.__version__)"

Run Python as uv run python … from now on.

You should see

Lockfile written to "…/renv.lock", plus a new renv/ folder and .Rprofile.

A new uv.lock and .venv/ folder, and a version number printed.

First commit, and a private copy on GitHub

What and why

git keeps the history of your project as a series of commits, snapshots with a message. GitHub keeps a copy of that history online, called a remote, so it survives a lost laptop and so others can clone it. A private repository is visible only to you and the people you invite. git status before the first commit matters: anything listed there will go to GitHub.

In the Terminal pane:

git init -b main
git status
git add -A
git commit -m "Example project: data, record, plan"
gh repo create fwe875-carbon-example --private --source=. --remote=origin --push
gh browse

You should see

A line saying the repository was created on GitHub, then the push. gh browse opens the repository in your browser, labelled Private, with your files and the data file.

If gh isn't installed or signed in

See Before class: install GitHub CLI, run gh auth login, then gh auth setup-git. Or create the repository on github.com (+ → New repository → Private, with no README), then run git remote add origin https://github.com/<you>/<repo>.git and git push -u origin main. If git asks for a password, it needs a personal access token, not your GitHub password; gh auth login avoids this.

Lab 2

Plan, audit, and tests first

individualCode tabskills

Goal: understand the plan well enough to check the code against it, let a skill check the data against the dictionary, and write the tests before any model code exists.

Read the plan, and question it

What and why

An analysis plan fixes the question, the data, the model, the calibration, and the figure before any results exist, so that choices made after seeing results can't steer the answer unnoticed. In this example the plan is written for you; in Part B you write your own. Reading it critically is the skill: you can only check code against a plan you understand.

  1. Read analysis_plan.md. In your note, answer: Why are calibration and validation split by whole years? Why only days with NEE_VUT_REF_QC ≥ 0.8? What do 0.0864 and 0.5 do in the PAR formula? What would make the plan's authors doubt their result (section 8)?
  2. Then ask Claude for a critique. Read it; change nothing in the plan (it belongs to the instructor here), but note any point you agree with for Lab 4.
Plan critique prompt
Read analysis_plan.md and CLAUDE.md. Don't open the data file and don't change anything.
List up to five specific ways this plan could give a misleading answer for this forest and these data (for example, the model's structure, the objective function, the period, the use of partitioned GPP and RECO), and any known-answer check that is missing. Tie each point to this plan, not to modelling in general.

Audit the data with the data-audit skill

What and why

A skill is a folder with a SKILL.md file. Its header holds a name and a description, which Claude always sees; the instructions below the header load only when a request matches the description, or when you pick the skill from the / menu. The data-audit skill writes and runs code that summarizes the data and compares them with the dictionary, so it works on files too big to read. It reports and changes nothing.
