Here it should also tell you whether the plan's choice of years holds up.

Ask in plain words
Are the files in data/raw ready to analyze? Include checks by year: rows per year, the mean of NEE_VUT_REF_QC per year, the annual sums of NEE_VUT_REF, GPP_NT_VUT_REF, and RECO_NT_VUT_REF, and the number of days with negative GPP_NT_VUT_REF.
  1. Claude should say it is using the data-audit skill. If it doesn't, pick /data-audit from the / menu.
  2. Approve the script it writes and runs (read the command first). It writes data_audit.md.
  3. Read the report. Does it support the plan's use of 2006–2014? Find the year that is mostly gap-filled, and the days where partitioned GPP is negative.
  4. Open .claude/skills/data-audit/SKILL.md in a text editor (the folder is hidden: on a Mac press Cmd+Shift+. in Finder). Find the description Claude matched, and the line that says it must never change data.
  5. Commit in the Terminal pane: git add -A, git commit -m "Data audit".

You should see

A table with PASS, PROBLEM, or CANNOT TELL for each check, with numbers as evidence: no duplicate or missing days, values within the dictionary's ranges, one year with mostly gap-filled NEE, a clear change in annual RECO and NEE between the early and later years, and some days with negative partitioned GPP.

Tests first: conversions, model behaviour, parameter recovery

What and why

A test is a short piece of code that checks one thing and reports pass or fail. Writing tests from the plan before the model exists means the rules come from the plan, not from whatever the code happens to produce. Parameter recovery is the most important one for any calibration: if the code can't find parameters you chose yourself in data you simulated, it can't be trusted to find them in real data. Putting the equations in one file of functions lets the tests and the analysis use exactly the same code.

Tests prompt
Write the tests in section 7 of analysis_plan.md, in tests/ (testthatpytest), before any model code.
The model will live in R/functions.Rpy/functions.py as small functions: PAR from shortwave, VPD in kPa, the temperature and VPD scalars, GPP, RECO, NEE, a calibration function, and the metrics in section 5. For now write only empty versions of these functions, each with a comment giving its inputs, outputs, and units.
For parameter recovery, read the 2006-2010 forcing from the raw data file, use the true values, noise, and seed in the plan, and require each parameter within 10%.
Run the tests and show me which fail. They should fail now, because the functions are empty.
  1. Read the parameter-recovery test yourself. Does it use the plan's true values, noise, and seed? Does it calibrate only on 2006–2010?
  2. Commit: git add -A, git commit -m "Tests from the plan".

Lab 3

Build, calibrate, and validate version 1

individualCode tabplan modegit

Goal: a complete first version that runs from the raw file to a figure with one command: the model, its calibration on 2006–2010, its validation on 2011–2014, and the metrics and figure in the plan. You review Claude's plan before any code exists, and the code before it runs.

Plan the build in plan mode

What and why

In plan mode Claude explores and describes what it will do, and changes nothing until you approve, so you can catch a wrong approach before code exists to hide it. Splitting the work into a preparation, a calibration, a validation, and a figure script, each listed in run_all, means one command reruns everything from the raw data, and each script can be checked on its own.

Choose Plan in the mode selector, then send:

Version 1 prompt (in plan mode)
Plan version 1 exactly as analysis_plan.md describes, as scripts added in order to run_all:
1. a preparation script: read the raw file, keep 2006-2014, convert units (PAR, VPD in kPa), mark calibration (2006-2010) and validation (2011-2014) years and the days with NEE_VUT_REF_QC >= 0.8, print how many days each step keeps or removes, and write data/processed/;
2. a calibration script: fill in the functions the tests call, calibrate the four parameters on the calibration days against daily NEE, and write outputs/parameters.csv with a column that flags any parameter at a bound;
3. a validation script: write outputs/metrics.csv (daily and monthly metrics, calibration and validation separately) and outputs/annual_nee.csv;
4. a figure script: outputs/figure1.png as in section 6, and outputs/figure1_caption.md with every number read from the output files.
Use the plan's start values, bounds, and fixed T_min and T_opt. Don't write code until I approve the plan.

Checkpoint before you approve

Does the plan use exactly the equations, units, bounds, years, and QC filter in analysis_plan.md? Is calibration kept away from 2011–2014? Is the caption generated from the outputs? If not, say what to change and ask for a new plan.

Approve, and read the code as it's written

What and why

The Code tab's diff view shows every line Claude changed, and lets you comment on single lines before the next step. The functions file holds the science: a sign error in NEE or a missed hPa-to-kPa conversion would pass silently into every result. Checking the equations against the plan line by line is the review only you can do quickly at this point.

  1. Approve the plan. Claude should still ask before each edit and command; if it starts editing without asking, switch the mode selector to Manual.
  2. When the functions file changes, click the change count (such as +60 −8) to open the diff view. Check each equation against section 3 of the plan: PAR, VPD in kPa, the temperature ramp with −5 and 20 °C, GPP, RECO, and NEE = RECO − GPP. Comment on at least one line you'd change or want explained, and send the comments (Cmd+Enter or Ctrl+Enter).
  3. When Claude says it's done, rerun everything and the tests yourself in the Terminal pane:
Rscript run_all.R
Rscript -e "testthat::test_dir('tests')"
uv run python run_all.py
uv run pytest -q

You should see

The preparation script prints the days kept and removed: 1,776 calibration days and 1,407 validation days with QC ≥ 0.8. The run writes outputs/, including session_info.txt; all tests pass, including parameter recovery. Different counts mean a filter or the year split differs from the plan.

Look at version 1 yourself, then tag it

What and why

Your own look comes before any agent's. A model can pass every test and still be scientifically wrong: the tests show the code does what the plan says, not that the plan's model describes the forest. Comparing your numbers with the class's is also a check: everyone ran the same plan on the same data, so large differences point to a mistake. A tag gives this commit a permanent name, v1, so you can say exactly which version a number came from.

  1. Open outputs/parameters.csv, outputs/metrics.csv, and outputs/figure1.png. In your note, answer: Did any parameter end at a bound, and what does section 8 of the plan say that means? Is validation much worse than calibration? Which months does the model get wrong?
  2. Compare your parameters with the class reference values the instructor shows. Small differences are normal; large ones are a finding.
  3. Add anything that looks wrong to review_log.md as round 0: IDs R0-1, R0-2, …, source "me".
  4. Record versions and tag, in the Terminal pane:
Rscript -e "renv::snapshot()"
git add -A
git commit -m "Version 1: model, calibration, validation, figure"
git tag -a v1 -m "First complete run"
git push
git push --tags

Lab 4

Review, decide, and improve: version 2

individualskills and agentsgit

Goal: one full review round on the example: three independent reviewer agents, your decision on every finding, an experiment and fixes on a branch, a comparison of what changed, and a tagged version 2.

Round 1: review

What and why

A review round collects every kind of check on one committed version: the tests, a full rerun, and three independent reviewers. Each reviewer is a subagent with its own context window: it starts from the files, not from your conversation, so it doesn't carry the assumptions built up while the code was written. Its tools can read but not edit, so a review can never change your project. The review-round skill runs the tests and the rerun itself, then hands the files to the three reviewers.

  1. In the Terminal pane: git status should say "nothing to commit", and git log --oneline --decorate -1 should show tag: v1.
  2. Pick /review-round from the / menu. Approve the commands it asks to run. The three reviewers then run at the same time, listed as background tasks; click one to see its output.
  3. Read the summary, then open review_log.md.

You should see

New rows numbered R1-1, R1-2, … with source, location, finding, and severity, and empty Decision and Reason columns. Your round-0 rows from Lab 3 are still there.

Decide on every finding yourself

What and why

Triage means sorting findings by what to do about them. It is the step that keeps the analysis yours: a reviewer can be wrong, can misread the science, or can suggest a change that quietly answers a different question. A written reason for each decision lets anyone see later why the analysis is the way it is.

  1. For every row, fill in Decision (accept, reject, or later) and Reason. Rejecting is fine; say why.
  2. You may ask Claude to explain a finding, but not to decide:
Explain a finding
Explain finding R1-[n] in review_log.md. Show me the evidence in the files: the lines of code, the rows of data, or the numbers in outputs/. Don't change anything, and don't tell me whether to accept it.
  1. If the review's rerun changed files in outputs/ (git status lists them), put them back with git restore outputs/: the review has already logged any change as a finding.
  2. Commit the decisions: git add review_log.md, git commit -m "Round 1: review and decisions".

An experiment: does the calibration target matter?

What and why

If Q10 ended at its upper bound in version 1 (it usually does), is the code wrong or the model? Your parameter-recovery test passed, so the code can find parameters when the model is right. NEE is a small difference between two large fluxes, so a model can match NEE with photosynthesis and respiration that are both wrong, as long as the errors cancel: equifinality. One way to test this is to calibrate against the partitioned GPP and RECO as well, which pins down each flux. That changes the plan, so it goes in deviations.md, with its own caveat: partitioned GPP and RECO are themselves estimated from NEE with a respiration model, so they are not independent measurements.

Make a branch for this round, in the Terminal pane:

git switch -c round-1
Experiment prompt
Add a second calibration to the calibration script, keeping the first: the same model, start values, and bounds, but minimizing the summed squared errors of daily GPP against GPP_NT_VUT_REF plus daily RECO against RECO_NT_VUT_REF, on the same calibration days. Write both parameter sets to outputs/parameters.csv with a column naming the objective, and both sets of metrics (NEE, GPP, and RECO; daily, monthly; calibration and validation) to outputs/metrics.csv. Add a test that the new objective uses only 2006-2010. Don't change anything else.
Then show me a table comparing the two parameter sets and their validation metrics.
  1. Compare the two: Which parameter set stays inside its bounds? Which objective predicts NEE better day by day, and which predicts GPP and RECO better? Do monthly and annual NEE tell the same story?
  2. Record the added calibration in deviations.md (what changed from the plan, why, and who decided), including which of the two version 2 reports as its main result, and your reason. Commit: git add -A, git commit -m "R1: second calibration objective; deviation recorded".

What to notice

Calibrating on NEE alone gives the better daily NEE but flux components far from the partitioned estimates, with Q10 pinned at its bound; calibrating on GPP and RECO moves every parameter off its bounds and matches the partitioned fluxes better, but gives worse daily NEE. Neither is "right": the model is missing processes (for example, snow cover or the slow recovery of photosynthesis in spring), and the choice of objective decides which errors you accept. Saying so is a result.

Fix the other accepted findings, test first

What and why

Fixing test first (a test that fails because of the problem, then the fix that makes it pass) proves the fix does what it claims and keeps the problem from coming back. One commit per finding, named by its ID, links every change in the code to a row in the review log. In class, fix at least one accepted finding; the rest can wait for the practice.

Fix prompt
Fix the findings in review_log.md marked accept that the calibration experiment didn't already address, one at a time, most serious first. For each one:
1. Where a test makes sense, add or change one that fails because of the problem, and show me it failing. (A README or caption fix may not need one: say so.)
2. Fix the code. Never edit data/raw/.
3. Run all the tests.
4. Commit with the message "R1-[n]: [short description]".
If a fix would change anything in analysis_plan.md, stop and ask me first; I'll record it in deviations.md. Don't fix anything that isn't marked accept.
At the end, fill in the "Fixed in" column of review_log.md from git log, and commit that.

Read each permission prompt, and each fix in the diff view. If a fix looks wrong, comment on the line before Claude commits.

See what changed, merge, and tag version 2

What and why

A diff shows exactly what changed between two versions; comparing outputs, not just code, shows whether a fix changed the result. git diff v1...round-1 (three dots) shows what the branch changed since v1, and GitHub's compare view shows the two figures side by side. Merging brings the branch into main; the tag v2 marks the end of the round.

Rscript run_all.R
Rscript -e "testthat::test_dir('tests')"
uv run python run_all.py
uv run pytest -q

If the rerun changed anything in outputs/, commit it (git add -A, git commit -m "Round 1: rerun outputs"). Then compare and push the branch:

git log --oneline v1..round-1
git diff v1...round-1 -- outputs/
git push -u origin round-1

Open https://github.com/<you>/fwe875-carbon-example/compare/v1...round-1 in your browser and scroll to the figure. Then merge and tag:

Rscript -e "renv::snapshot()"
git add renv.lock
git commit -m "Record package versions"

If git says there is nothing to commit, that's fine.

git switch main
git merge round-1
git tag -a v2 -m "After review round 1"
git push
git push --tags

You should see

On GitHub, the tags v1 and v2, the round's commits on main named by finding ID, and a deviations.md entry for the second calibration objective. That is the whole loop, once. Part B starts it on your own project.

Part B · Your project

Start your own project

Tests, version 1, and review rounds continue in the practice after Week 2. Each step names the example step it repeats, so look back when you need the details.

fwe875-week2-kit.zipThe starter kit for your own project: the same skeleton, skills, and reviewer agents, with templates to fill in
fire_regions_class_dataset.csvOnly if you have no data of your own: the class dataset, 10 fire regions × 20 seasons (synthetic)

Make your project from the kit

What and why

The kit has the same skeleton as the example, with templates in place of the example's filled-in files. The same layout means the kit's skills and reviewer agents find your data, plan, and outputs where they expect them, and a classmate can find their way around your project.

As in Lab 1, step 1, but into a folder called my-project:

macOS

mkdir -p ~/Documents/fwe875/my-project
cp -R ~/Downloads/fwe875-week2-kit/. ~/Documents/fwe875/my-project/
cd ~/Documents/fwe875/my-project

Windows PowerShell

Expand-Archive $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-week2-kit.zip -DestinationPath $HOME\Downloads -Force
mkdir $HOME\Documents\fwe875\my-project
Copy-Item -Recurse -Force $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-week2-kit\* $HOME\Documents\fwe875\my-project
cd $HOME\Documents\fwe875\my-project

Delete the files for the language you don't use, as in Lab 1. KIT.md explains every file.

Put your data in, and record where they came from

What and why

In the example, the data record was written for you. For your own data you write it: provenance (source, citation, license or terms, date, checksum) and caveats. Writing it before any analysis also makes you answer a question that matters for everything after: may these data go to GitHub and to a classmate?

  1. Copy your data files into data/raw/ with Finder or File Explorer. (No data of your own: move the class dataset there.)
  2. Get each file's checksum (shasum -a 256 data/raw/* or Get-FileHash data\raw\* -Algorithm SHA256).
  3. Fill in data/README.md by hand, one section per file, using the example's data/README.md as a model.
  4. If a file is over about 50 MB, or may not go to GitHub, add its path to .gitignore now, and write in data/README.md how someone else would get it.

Write CLAUDE.md, and check the project's rules

What and why

CLAUDE.md is the project's standing brief: Claude Code reads it at the start of every session, and so does every reviewer agent. Its data dictionary defines what correct data look like, so it is what the data audit and the reviewers check against; anything you leave out, they can't check. The example's CLAUDE.md shows the level of detail that works: units, ranges, missing-value codes, and what counts as one independent observation.

  1. Open CLAUDE.md in a text editor. Replace each <placeholder>: your project paragraph, your data dictionary (from your Before-class draft), how your observations are grouped, and your language.
  2. Start a new Code tab session in my-project and repeat Lab 1, step 3: Manual mode, the kit's skills in the / list, and the deny-rule test.
  3. Ask for a critique of your dictionary, then fix it yourself:
Dictionary critique prompt
Read CLAUDE.md and data/README.md. Don't open the data files and don't change anything.
List anything in my data dictionary or my description of how the data are grouped that is ambiguous or missing, as questions for me: units, missing-value codes, time zones, coordinate systems, scale factors, IDs, and what counts as one independent observation.

Software versions, first commit, private GitHub copy

What and why

The same three habits as Lab 1, steps 4 and 5: a lockfile so the versions travel with the code, a first commit before Claude writes any code, and a private remote copy. Read git status before the first commit: with your own data, this is where a file that may not be shared would slip out.

Rscript -e "renv::init()"
uv add pandas numpy matplotlib pytest

Add the packages your data need, e.g. scipy, geopandas, rasterio, xarray.

git init -b main
git status
git add -A
git commit -m "Project skeleton, data, and data dictionary"
gh repo create fwe875-my-project --private --source=. --remote=origin --push

You may replace fwe875-my-project with a short name of your own, without spaces.

Audit your data

What and why

The same skill as Lab 2, now on data nobody has checked for you. Few datasets come back with no problems. Each PROBLEM row is a decision for you, not for the skill: fix it in a cleaning script, drop it with a printed count, or keep it and say why. A data audit that finds nothing usually means a thin data dictionary.

  1. Ask: "Are the files in data/raw ready to analyze?" (or pick /data-audit). Approve the script it runs.
  2. Confirm one PROBLEM row yourself, from the numbers it shows.
  3. Answer its "Questions for the researcher" by updating your dictionary in CLAUDE.md. Commit: git add -A, git commit -m "Data audit".

Write your plan and definition of done (no AI)

What and why

The example's plan showed what a plan needs: the question, the data and what counts as one observation, the model or test, how it will be checked on data it hasn't seen (if it is fitted), the figure, known-answer checks, and what would make you doubt the result. You write yours yourself, before results, so that the choices are yours and can't drift toward whatever the data happen to show. Your definition of done is your stopping rule for the review rounds.

  1. Open analysis_plan.md and fill it in from your Before-class draft, using the example's plan as a model.
  2. Choose at least two known-answer checks that fit your analysis, for example:
    • any fitted model: simulate data from it with parameters you choose, and check the code recovers them (as in the example)
    • summaries: one site's, plot's, or pixel's value calculated by hand
    • rasters: a tiny synthetic raster with known values, run through your processing
    • time series: a known seasonal cycle plus a known trend
    • published data: one number from the paper that published the dataset
  3. Open done.md. Delete what doesn't apply; add at least one item specific to your data.
  4. Ask for a critique (the plan critique prompt from Lab 2, step 1, works here too), revise the plan yourself, then commit: git add analysis_plan.md done.md, git commit -m "Analysis plan and definition of done".

Is there a published skill for your project?

What and why

Researchers and companies publish skills on GitHub, often dozens in one collection. A good one carries expertise you don't have; a poor one adds rules that conflict with yours, installs software you didn't ask for, or sends your queries to outside services. Nobody reviews them for you: Anthropic's own skills collection says its skills are "for demonstration and educational purposes" and should be tested before you rely on them. Vetting means reading what a skill will make Claude do before it can do it. Deciding not to add one, with a reason, is as good an outcome as adding it.

Seven skills we read in October 2026, chosen to show the range
Skill and sourceWhat it doesWhat we noticed reading it
scientific-problem-selection
Anthropic, life-sciences collection		Walks you through choosing and stress-testing a research problem (after Fischbach and Walsh, Cell, 2024): ideas, risks, success measures, what to do when a project is stuckInstructions and reference notes only, no code. Written for life scientists, but the framework is general. Fits your Week 1 question, and works well in the Claude app
literature-review
K-Dense Scientific Agent Skills (MIT license)		A systematic review pipeline: database searches, screening counts for a PRISMA diagram, synthesis, citation checks, a PDF reportIts setup installs a third-party search tool by piping a script from the internet into the shell and signing in to that service; it also wants LaTeX and, optionally, an OpenRouter API key. Your queries leave your computer. Compare with your Week 1 Zotero workflow before adopting it
statistical-analysis
K-Dense		Test selection, assumption checks, effect sizes, Bayesian alternatives, APA-style reportingPython only, with package versions left unpinned. It tells Claude to cite a particular paper when writing references: a skill can steer what Claude writes
scientific-visualization
K-Dense		Honest, accessible figures: encodings that don't mislead, colorblind-safe palettes, uncertainty shown, export with a provenance filePython only; versions pinned. Says plainly that it is not a journal-compliance check. Useful again in Week 4
geopandas

skill-creator
Anthropic skills collection		Helps you draft a new skill, test it on example requests, and tune its description so it loads when it shouldRuns scripts and extra Claude sessions for its tests, which uses your plan's limits. For writing your own skill after class

Other collections exist for most fields; searching GitHub for "SKILL.md" plus your method finds them. Many research skills are written for Python: for an R project, that alone can be a reason to say no.

  1. Pick the one skill, from the table or a collection in your field, closest to your project. Read its SKILL.md and its folder's file list on GitHub, and fill in the checklist in your note:
Skill vetting checklist
## Vetting: [skill name]
Source (URL) and version or commit:
License:
1. When does it load? Would its description match requests where I don't want it?
2. Which tools does it pre-approve (allowed-tools), so they run without asking you? Bash? Write?
3. What does it run or install? Anything piped from the internet into the shell?
4. What leaves my computer? Web searches, APIs, accounts, API keys?
5. Does it fit my project: language, data type, package versions?
6. Does anything conflict with my CLAUDE.md or the course rules?
Decision: add to my project / use in the Claude app / don't use
Because:
  1. If you decide to add it, copy only that skill's folder into your project (this example adds geopandas; use your skill's folder name). The clone goes in skill-sources, outside your project:

macOS

git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/K-Dense-AI/scientific-agent-skills.git ~/Documents/fwe875/skill-sources/k-dense
cp -R ~/Documents/fwe875/skill-sources/k-dense/skills/geopandas .claude/skills/

Windows PowerShell

git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/K-Dense-AI/scientific-agent-skills.git $HOME\Documents\fwe875\skill-sources\k-dense
Copy-Item -Recurse $HOME\Documents\fwe875\skill-sources\k-dense\skills\geopandas .claude\skills\

For Anthropic's collections: git clone --depth 1 https://github.com/anthropics/life-sciences.git (the skill is the scientific-problem-selection folder) or https://github.com/anthropics/skills.git (skills/skill-creator), into skill-sources in the same way.

  1. Type / in your session: the skill should be listed (if not, start a new session). Test it with one request that should load it and one that shouldn't.
  2. Record where it came from: a "Skills from others" line in README.md (name, URL, commit, license) and a row in your AI-use log. Commit: git add .claude README.md, git commit -m "Add [skill] skill from [source]".

Decided against it? Keep the checklist with your reason in your note; that is a complete result.

Two other ways skills arrive

Plugins: in the Code tab, click + next to the prompt box → Plugins → Add plugin to browse plugins from your marketplaces, including Anthropic's official one. Choose project or local scope rather than user scope (all your projects on this computer), and read what it will install: one plugin can add skills, agents, hooks, and connectors, and every enabled plugin adds to the context of every message. Your Claude account: skills for thinking and writing rather than code, such as scientific-problem-selection, can also be added to your Claude account under Customize → Skills in the app's sidebar, for use in chat and Cowork. For your project, add the skill to the project folder as above, so git records exactly what shaped the analysis.

Next: tests and version 1 (in class if there's time)

What and why

From here your project follows Labs 2 to 4 of the example: tests from your plan before any analysis code, version 1 built in plan mode and checked in the diff view, your own look, a tag, then review rounds until your definition of done is met. The prompts below are the example's, made general.

Tests prompt (your project)
Write tests in tests/ (testthatpytest) before any analysis code:
1. Data tests: every rule in the data dictionary in CLAUDE.md that the cleaned data must meet (ranges, units, IDs, missing-value codes, how rows are grouped). Point them at the cleaned files in data/processed/, which don't exist yet.
2. Known-answer tests: the checks listed in analysis_plan.md. The analysis steps will live in functions in R/functions.Rpy/functions.py that the tests call with simulated or hand-calculated data; for now, write only empty functions with a comment saying what each must return. Set a seed for any simulation and take the true values from the plan.
Run the tests and show me which fail. They should fail now.
Version 1 prompt (your project, in plan mode)
Plan version 1 of the analysis in analysis_plan.md, as scripts added in order to run_all:
- a cleaning script that reads data/raw/ and writes data/processed/: one commented fix for each PROBLEM in data_audit.md that I have decided to fix (ask me which), each printing how many rows, records, or pixels it changes;
- an analysis script that fills in the functions the tests call, fits or computes what the plan says, and writes estimates with uncertainty and n (at the level of the unit of replication) to outputs/;
- a figure script that makes outputs/figure1.png as planned, and writes outputs/figure1_caption.md with every number read from the output files, never typed.
If the data are large, develop on a small subset first and tell me how you made it.
Don't write code until I approve the plan.

Then read the code in the diff view, rerun everything and the tests yourself, look at the outputs, log round-0 findings, and tag v1, exactly as in Lab 3, steps 2–3.

Guidelines for any project

Your project differs from your neighbor's, but the same problems turn up again and again. Use this section while you work: it is what the reviewers and your own checks should look for.

Common problems by type of data

DataProblems to look forA check that catches them
Tables
field plots, surveys, lab measurements		Units that change between sources or years; IDs that don't match across files; missing-value codes read as numbers; duplicate rows; repeated measurements treated as independentRanges split by source and period; rows per ID; rows that fail to join between files
Time series
sensors, weather, flux towers, streamflow		Time zones and daylight saving; gaps, repeated timestamps, fill values; leap days and 365-day model calendars; water year vs. calendar year; autocorrelationRows per day and per year; a plot of the whole series; the first and last timestamps
Rasters and imagery
Landsat, MODIS, climate grids		Different coordinate systems or resolutions; scale factors and offsets not applied; nodata values and cloud masks; resampling method; pixel area in degree grids; neighboring pixels aren't independentPrint the coordinate system, resolution, extent, and value range of every layer; overlay one layer on a map
Points and records
GBIF, telemetry, plot locations		Swapped or truncated coordinates; datum; duplicates; uneven sampling effort; synonyms in namesMap every point; count records per grid cell or observer; match names against a reference list
Model output
simulations, ensembles, CMIP		Units and variable names that differ between models; spin-up years; ensemble members treated as independent replicates; different calendars and gridsOne table of units per model; each model's mean plotted through time

Large data

  • Never let Claude read a big file whole. It fills the context window and uses up your plan's limits. Ask for code that summarizes the file instead: dimensions, types, ranges, missing counts, a few rows.
  • Develop on a subset. Have a script cut a small, representative piece into data/processed/. Build and test on it, then run the finished pipeline on the full data.
  • Keep files over about 50 MB out of git (list them in .gitignore), and say in data/README.md exactly how someone else gets them.
  • Run long jobs yourself in the Code tab's Terminal pane or your own terminal, rather than asking Claude to run them and wait.

What reviewer agents are good and bad at

Usually good at

  • Reading code for rows lost silently, wrong filters, and mismatches with the plan
  • Checking numbers in captions and READMEs against the output files
  • Spotting misleading figures: mislabelled axes, hidden data, confusing color
  • Listing missing tests and unrecorded choices

Weak at

  • Data they can't open: large, binary, or compressed files (the reviewers read files; they don't run code)
  • Your field's conventions, unless CLAUDE.md states them
  • Whether a result is scientifically plausible, and what is missing from the data
  • Giving the same answer twice; they can also be confidently wrong, and they share blind spots with the model that wrote the code

When to stop

Stop when every box in your done.md is ticked and the last round found nothing new you would call "must fix". Reviewers will always find something to suggest; past that point, more rounds cost time and usage without changing the result. Mark the rest "later" in the review log, with a reason. Each round runs three agents, which takes a noticeable share of a Pro plan's limits: check the usage ring next to the model picker, and fix things between rounds rather than reviewing the same version twice.

Exit ticket

Answer in a note of your own (for example, in your vault's 40_Projects folder) before you leave. Nothing is submitted: the instructor asks a few people to share an answer in the debrief, and you'll use your answers when you continue in the practice.

  1. In the example, which parameter ended at a bound in version 1, and what did the second calibration tell you about why?
  2. One review finding you rejected, and your reason.
  3. The first problem the data audit found in your own data, and what you'll do about it.
  4. The known-answer check you'll write first for your own project.
  5. The published skill you looked at: did you add it, and what decided it?

Next week

Nothing to prepare. Week 3 starts with writing a short draft in class, without AI, about your own results or about the carbon example. Keep your project and your carbon-example folder. Optional: the Week 2 practice (tests, version 1, and review rounds on your own project until your definition of done is met; a partner's clean rerun and code review; a skill of your own), and the readings.

Week 3 · Research stage: writing and review

Writing and review

Writing is thinking. Don’t outsource it.

A good paper tells one clear story. This week uses the main principles of science writing, from Joshua Schimel's book Writing Science. You start by writing a short draft in Microsoft Word, without AI. Then Claude reads a text copy of your Word file and checks your draft against these principles. You edit one pass at a time, and you make every change in Word. You keep your citations in Zotero, and you check each one. Then you review manuscripts with a panel of reviewer subagents, and you compare the panel with real referees and with a classmate. Last, you check a proposal against the criteria of its call.

In this session

  • Start: Write first, without AI
  • Concepts: Best practice for science writing · Writing with Claude · Proposals
  • Your writing: Lab 1: Find your story · Lab 2: Edit in passes · Lab 3: Citations and references
  • Review: Lab 4: Review with a panel of agents · Lab 5: Review a classmate's draft by hand
  • Proposals: Lab 6: Check a proposal

Start of class

Write first, without AI

individualWordno AI

Goal: write your story and a short first draft in Word, without AI. Keep this draft as your own version. All the labs of today start from it.

Why we start here

Writing is thinking. When you write, you find out what you know, what you claim, and what is missing (Schimel, pp. 4–6). If AI writes the first draft, you lose this thinking, and the text is not yours. Thus, for the next 35 minutes, close Claude. A rough draft is good: you improve it in all the later labs.

Choose your topic. Get the templates

What and why

You write about results that you already have. If your own project has results in outputs/, write about them. If it does not, write about the carbon example from Week 2. Its results are in carbon-example/outputs/. The writing kit has two templates: story.md for your story, and draft.docx, a Word file with the section headings in place. Claude uses these headings later to find your sections.

  1. Download fwe875-writing-kit.zip from the Toolkit. On a Mac, Safari unzips it into Downloads. With a different browser, double-click the zip.
  2. Open a terminal in the folder of your project, for example carbon-example.
  3. Type these commands:

macOS

mkdir -p manuscript
cp ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/manuscript/story.md manuscript/
cp ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/manuscript/draft.docx manuscript/
ls manuscript

Windows PowerShell

Expand-Archive $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit.zip -DestinationPath $HOME\Downloads -Force
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force manuscript | Out-Null
Copy-Item $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\manuscript\story.md manuscript\
Copy-Item $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\manuscript\draft.docx manuscript\
Get-ChildItem manuscript

You should see

Two files in manuscript/: story.md and draft.docx.

Write your story in four answers

What and why

A paper is one story. Before you write sentences, decide what the story is. These four answers are your plan for the draft. In Lab 1, you compare them with the story that Claude reads in your draft. Take about 8 minutes.

  1. Open manuscript/story.md in a text editor.
  2. Answer each question in one or two sentences:
    • Opening: What larger problem does your work help to solve? Who cares about it?
    • Challenge: What is not known? Write your question as "To learn X, we did Y."
    • Action: What are your two or three key results? Give the size and the unit of each.
    • Resolution: What do these results tell us about the problem in the opening?
  3. Write your main message in two sentences.
  4. Name your reader and a target journal. Find the AI policy of that journal for authors. Keep the link.
  5. Save the file.

Put your sources in Zotero

What and why

You add each citation from Zotero, not by typing it. Then Zotero formats it, and the kit can check it later against the record of the publisher. Two to four sources are sufficient for this short draft. Take about 5 minutes.

  1. In Zotero, make a collection for your draft.
  2. Add each source by its DOI. Click the magic wand (Add Item by Identifier). Paste the DOI.
  3. In Word, open manuscript/draft.docx. On the Zotero tab, click Document Preferences. Select the style of your target journal. Keep Store Citations As: Fields.
Sources for the carbon example
  • The dataset: Pastorello et al. (2020), 10.1038/s41597-020-0534-3
  • What eddy covariance measures: Baldocchi (2003), 10.1046/j.1365-2486.2003.00629.x
  • Light-use efficiency: Monteith (1972), 10.2307/2401901
  • Partitioning NEE into GPP and RECO: Reichstein et al. (2005), 10.1111/j.1365-2486.2005.001002.x
  • Weather and carbon uptake at Davos: Zielis et al. (2014), 10.5194/bg-11-1627-2014

Read the abstract of each source before you cite it.

If Word has no Zotero tab

Open Zotero. Select Settings → Cite → Word Processors. Reinstall the Word add-in. Then restart Word. Zotero must be open while you add citations.

Write a short draft in Word

What and why

This draft is short: approximately 300 words. It has a title, a short paragraph for each section, and two to four citations. It does not have to be good. It has to be yours. Take about 20 minutes.

  1. In draft.docx, replace the text in [brackets] under each heading. Keep the headings.
  2. Write a title that states your finding.
  3. Write an Introduction that ends with your question: "To learn X, we did Y."
  4. Write three to five sentences of Methods.
  5. Write a Results paragraph. Use only numbers from outputs/, with their units.
  6. Write a Discussion paragraph that answers your question.
  7. Add each citation with Zotero → Add/Edit Citation. Do not type citations.
  8. Under the References heading, click Zotero → Add/Edit Bibliography.
  9. Delete the template's note at the end. Save the file.

Keep this draft as your own version

What and why

The file draft-0.docx is a copy of the draft that you wrote alone. The git tag draft-0 marks the same version. At the end of Lab 2, you compare your edited draft with this copy. The comparison shows how much of the text is still yours. It is also the evidence for your AI-use statement. The line ~$* stops git from saving the lock files that Word makes, such as ~$draft.docx.

  1. Save the draft in Word. Close Word.
  2. In the terminal, type these commands:

macOS

cp manuscript/draft.docx manuscript/draft-0.docx
printf '\n~$*\n' >> .gitignore
git add .gitignore manuscript
git commit -m "Draft 0, written without AI"
git tag -a draft-0 -m "My draft before any AI"

Windows PowerShell

Copy-Item manuscript\draft.docx manuscript\draft-0.docx
Add-Content .gitignore @('', '~$*')
git add .gitignore manuscript
git commit -m "Draft 0, written without AI"
git tag -a draft-0 -m "My draft before any AI"
  1. If your project is on GitHub, also type git push. Then type git push --tags.
  2. Do not change draft-0.docx again.

Best practice for science writing

These principles come from Joshua Schimel's book Writing Science (2012). We give them in our own words, with the page numbers of the book. Schimel makes a difference between principles and rules. A rule, such as "use the active voice", usually helps. A good writer can break a rule for a good reason. A principle is different: you never break it. The first principle is: make the work of the reader easy. The author must make the text clear, not the reader (p. 5).

A paper is a story

A science paper is a story about how nature works. You tell this story with your data. Its characters can be trees, microbes, or carbon fluxes. A good story has one main message. Two or three key results support this message (pp. 8–19). The main tool of the book is the OCAR structure:

  1. Openingthe larger problem, and who cares about it
  2. Challengethe specific gap, and your question: "To learn X, we did Y"
  3. Actionwhat you did and what you found
  4. Resolutionthe answer, and what it changes for the problem in the opening

Journals ask for the IMRaD layout: Introduction, Methods, Results, and Discussion. IMRaD is a rule. OCAR is the story in the layout, and it is a principle (p. 32). Readers think that the Opening, the Challenge, and the Resolution tell the point of your paper. Thus these three parts must carry your message without the other parts (p. 30).

Story partWhere it goes in a paperWhat it must do
Title and abstractThe first linesTell the full story in a small space. Give the finding, not only the topic
OpeningThe first paragraph of the IntroductionSet the direction and the scope. Start where your story starts, not with textbook facts (pp. 35–47)
ChallengeThe end of the IntroductionLead the reader to one specific gap, as a funnel does. Then state the question (pp. 50–66)
ActionMethods, Results, most of the DiscussionGive a purpose for each method. Start each result with its pattern and its size (pp. 67–82)
ResolutionThe end of the Discussion, or the ConclusionsAnswer the question at the scope of the opening. Say what the answer means (pp. 83–94)

Nine principles, from the full paper to the word

LevelPrincipleTest it with one questionPages
StoryTell one story: one main message and two or three key results.Can you say the message in two sentences?8–19
StoryUse OCAR. Put each part of the story in its correct place.Do the Opening, Challenge, and Resolution alone give the point?26–34
Opening and challengeGive the direction at the start. Lead to one specific gap. State the question. Show the problem before the method.Is there a sentence such as "To learn X, we did Y"?35–66
ActionGive a purpose for each method. In the results, give the pattern first, then its size. Keep the data apart from your interpretation.Does each key result have a size, not only a p-value?67–82
ResolutionAnswer the question at the scope of the opening. Give a synthesis, not a repeat of the results. Do not add new ideas. Give the limits of the work early. End on what you showed.Does the last paragraph answer the question of the Introduction?83–94, 180–188
ParagraphsMake one point in each paragraph. Put the point at the start or at the end.Can you write the point of each paragraph in one line?95–111
SentencesPut old information first (the topic position). Put new or important information last (the stress position). Keep the subject short, and put the verb near it. Link each sentence to the sentence before it.Is the key word of each sentence at its end?112–132
WordsPut actions in verbs, not in nouns (nominalizations). Use the active voice when you can. Use short, common words. Define terms for your reader. Use numbers, not words such as "dramatically". Cut 10–20% of a first draft.Can a reader from a different field understand it?133–173
ProcessEdit from the top down: structure, then clarity, then flow, then language (SCFL). Do more than one round. Read the text aloud.Did you correct the story before the sentences?174–179
Six common faults, and how to correct them (our examples)
FaultExampleA better version
A gap that is not specific (p. 54)"Little is known about how well simple models work.""Simple light-use-efficiency models are calibrated on net CO2 exchange, but we do not know if they also get photosynthesis and respiration right at a cold subalpine site."
The researcher, not the finding, is the subject (p. 56)"Author et al. (2019) found that cover crops halve nitrate losses.""Cover crops can halve nitrate losses (Author et al. 2019)."
Significance with no size (pp. 76–79)"Buffers significantly reduced nitrate export (p = 0.01).""Buffers reduced nitrate export by about 40% (p = 0.01)."
An action in a noun (pp. 140–143)"We conducted an analysis of the soil cores.""We analyzed the soil cores."
An end that makes the result weak (pp. 91–92, 180–181)"…but the model has limitations, and more research is needed.""Although the record covers one site, it shows that …"
A paragraph with no point (p. 109)Five sentences about snow, drought, spring, and model sizeDecide the one point. Write it as the first sentence. Move or cut the other sentences.

Writing with Claude

Claude reads fast, and it is a good copy editor. It also writes fluent text, but it does not care about your argument. Use Claude to see your draft as a reader sees it. Keep the thinking for yourself.

LevelClaude canOnly you canIn the kit
StoryFind the Opening, Challenge, Action, and Resolution. Say what it thinks your message and question are. Find promises that no result deliversDecide the message and the question. Decide what to cutstory-check
ParagraphsMake a reverse outline: the point of each paragraph, and where the point is. Find broken links and terms that changeChange the order of paragraphs, and write them againflow-check
Sentences and wordsFind long subjects, hidden verbs, vague words, weak hedges, and missing numbers. Suggest numbered edits, each with a reasonAccept or reject each edit. Type it in Word. Make sure that the meaning stays the sameline-edit
CitationsMake sure that each source exists and agrees with its DOI record. Find the passage that supports a sentenceRead the source. Decide if it supports your claimcite-check
ReviewFour reviewer subagents read the paper at the same time. Each has its own checklistAct as the editor. Decide on each point, and give a reasonfour reviewer subagents
ProposalsCompare a proposal with its call and the review criteria. Find claims with no support and risks with no planDecide what to propose and why it matters. Write every sentenceproposal-check

Your manuscript stays in Word

Most journals and co-authors work with Word files. Claude Code cannot read a Word file directly. Thus the kit's word-text skill makes a text copy, for example draft.txt, next to your Word file. The copy gives each paragraph a number (¶). It also marks each Zotero citation, comment, and piece of hidden text. It shows tracked changes as if you accepted them. The skills and the reviewers read the copy and report. They never change your Word file. You make each change in Word.

Code tab, with the writing kit (in class)Claude for Word (optional add-in)Chat
What Claude readsThe text copy that the word-text skill makesThe open document, with its comments and tracked changesAn uploaded Word file, as text only
How a change gets into WordYou type it, with Track Changes onClaude writes it as a tracked change. You accept or reject it in WordYou type it
Skills and reviewer subagents of the kitYesNoNo
Record of your promptsprompt-log.jsonlWrite them in your AI-use logWrite them in your AI-use log
Be carefulSave the Word file before each check. Claude reads the last saved versionUse it only on documents that you trust: hidden text can give it instructions. Test it first on a copy with Zotero citationsComments and tracked changes are possibly not read

Track Changes and Compare keep you in control. Turn on Track Changes (Review → Track Changes) before you edit. Word then shows each change, with your name. Review → Compare compares draft-0.docx with draft.docx, and it marks every change in a third document. This comparison shows how much of the text is still yours. It is also the evidence for your AI-use statement.

Seven rules for writing with Claude

  1. Write the first draft yourself. Then keep a copy. Writing shows you what you think (pp. 4–6). The file draft-0.docx and the git tag draft-0 are a record of what you wrote alone.
  2. Edit from the top down, with one pass for each prompt. Good sentences cannot repair a broken story.
  3. Ask for a diagnosis before a fix. With a list of problems, you make the fix. A full rewrite replaces your thinking and your voice.
  4. Claude never adds facts, numbers, citations, or interpretations. Reject each edit that adds one. Claude can make references that look real but do not exist (a fabricated citation).
  5. You decide each change. Look at each change. In class, you type each change in Word, with Track Changes on. With the optional add-in, Claude inserts the edits that you chose, and you accept or reject each one. Compare shows all changes since draft-0. One moved word can change a claim (pp. 114–115).
  6. Keep your voice. Reject edits that do not sound like you. Look for common AI habits:
    • big words of praise, such as "crucial" or "novel insights";
    • many hedges in one sentence;
    • endings such as "further research is needed".
    The voice mode of line-edit finds these habits in your own text.
  7. Obey the rules of your journal or funder, and keep a record. The prompt log keeps the prompts that you send. Your commits and the Word comparison keep each change. With these records, you can write an accurate AI-use statement.

How to ask: seven patterns that work

Published guides and studies agree on a small set of prompt patterns. The kit's skills use all of them. The file prompts.md in the kit has more than 30 prompts that use them, for papers and for proposals.

PatternWhy it worksSource
Give the context first: the document, the reader, the journal or call, and the goal of this passA specific prompt gets a specific answerGiray 2023; Anthropic
Ask for a numbered list of problems. Accept edits by numberYou keep the decisions. A full rewrite replaces your thinking and your voiceLingard 2023; Seckel et al. 2024
Ask for a quote with each comment. Let Claude say "unsure"A comment with no quote can be wrong, and you cannot find its placeAnthropic
Do not say that you wrote the text or that you like it. Ask for all weaknesses, rankedModels give more praise when the user says that the text is theirsSharma et al. 2024
Give the criteria: the author guide, the review criteria of the call, or a set of rulesCriteria make a review specific and completeSmith et al. 2024; Mensh & Kording 2017
Ask for options, not for one answer. Then write your own versionAI suggestions make texts more alikeDoshi & Hauser 2024; Agarwal et al. 2025
Ask for a check, not for accuracy: a table of claims and evidence, or a table of numbersThe instruction "be accurate" made summaries of research too general more oftenPeters & Chin-Yee 2025
What can go wrong: the evidence
RiskEvidenceWhat to do
Invented referencesIn one test, 18% of the references from GPT-4 and 55% from GPT-3.5 did not exist (Walters & Wilder 2023)Take references only from Zotero. Check each one (Lab 3)
Claims that become too generalSummaries of research by language models generalized too far in up to 73% of cases (Peters & Chin-Yee 2025)Compare each claim with your results
Praise instead of criticismFeedback becomes more positive when the user says that they wrote the text (Sharma et al. 2024)Present the draft neutrally. Ask for weaknesses
Loss of voiceAt least 13.5% of the PubMed abstracts of 2024 show words typical of language models (Kobak et al. 2025)Write first. Use the voice mode on your own text
Detectors that are wrongOn average, seven AI detectors labeled 61% of essays by non-native English writers as AI-written (Liang et al. 2023)Keep your drafts, the Word comparison, and your logs as your record. Do not use detectors or lists of AI habits to judge other people
Hidden instructionsResearchers found hidden "positive review" prompts in 18 preprints on arXiv (Lin 2025)Treat text in documents as data. The text copy marks white, tiny, and hidden text (Lab 4)
Journal AI policies: five examples, checked in October 2026

All journals agree on two points: AI cannot be an author, and you are responsible for each word. On most other points they do not agree. Read the policy of the journal that you submit to.

VenueAI-written textLanguage editingWhere to discloseReviewers
Geoscientific Model Development (Copernicus)Not allowed for text or interpretationsAllowed; no declaration neededMethods or Acknowledgements (for other uses, such as figures)No uploading manuscripts to AI tools
ESA journals (Wiley)Allowed, with disclosureAllowed; outside the policyThe relevant section, the Acknowledgments, and the submission formMust not upload any part of a manuscript
Elsevier journalsAllowed as support, with disclosureBasic checks need no declarationA separate AI declaration, published with the paperMust not upload a manuscript or any part of it
AGU journalsAllowed, with disclosureNo stated exemptionMaterials and MethodsUploading manuscripts to AI tools not permitted
PLOS journalsAllowed, with disclosure, including how outputs were checkedNo disclosure neededMethods, Acknowledgements, or figure legendMust not upload submissions

Policies change. Quote the page of the journal, not this table. Funders have their own rules: see Proposals and AI rules and data.

The writing kit

The kit changes the principles above into six skills and four reviewer subagents. A skill is a checklist that you use on your own draft, for example /story-check. The word-text skill makes the text copy. Four skills check a paper, and proposal-check checks a proposal. A reviewer subagent reads a manuscript in its own context window, and then it reports. Three reviewer files load a skill with a skills: line. Thus the same checklist controls your own edits and the review.

The tools: line of each reviewer has only tools that read. Claude Code enforces this line, so a reviewer cannot change your files. No skill changes your Word file. Three skills write a report to the folder reviews/. The story, clarity, and methods reviewers give each point in the same parts: where, observation, evidence, why it matters, and the requested action. The citation reviewer gives a table of sources and a table of support.

Ideas from open collections of writing skills went into the kit. The README of the kit names them. Before you install a collection of skills, read its files. Some collections write whole papers or proposals for you. One popular collection tells Claude to add its authors' own paper to your references.

Proposals

A proposal tells the same type of story as a paper, but for a different reader. A panel of busy people from several subfields reads it. It competes with other good proposals. Schimel gives these principles:

  • Many reviewers decide "no" or "maybe" by the end of the introduction. A good design does not save a dull question (pp. 31–32).
  • Write for the panel. Start from a problem that most panel members care about. Then narrow to your work (p. 43).
  • Ask questions, not only objectives or tasks (pp. 58–60).
  • Make each hypothesis testable. Use a concrete verb. State a result that could show it false (p. 139).
  • End with a paragraph that brings the parts together and makes the case for the work (pp. 92–93).
  • A proposal usually needs one panelist who argues for it. Give that person the words (p. 24).

Rules for AI in proposals

FunderApplicantsReviewers
NIHNIH does not consider an application original if AI substantially developed it, or a section of it. NIH can use detection tools. Each principal investigator can submit at most six applications in a calendar year (NOT-OD-25-132, from 25 September 2025)Reviewers must not use generative AI on applications or critiques (NOT-OD-23-149)
NSFNSF encourages proposers to say in the Project Description whether and how they used generative AI. The proposer is responsible for the content. Since December 2025, the NSF definition of research misconduct names AI-based tools (PAPPG 24-1, Supplement 1)Reviewers must not put proposal content into AI tools that NSF has not approved
Your department, fellowship, or universityAsk your advisor or the program officer. Rules for dissertation proposals and internal grants are differentTreat each proposal that you review as confidential

Thus, in this course, Claude never drafts a proposal. It compares your text with the call and its review criteria, and it asks the questions that a panel would ask. You write every sentence.

Review criteria are your checklist

FrameworkWhat reviewers judgeIn proposal-check
NSF, and calls that use its criteria, such as the GRFPTwo criteria: Intellectual Merit and Broader Impacts. For both: the potential to advance knowledge or to benefit society; creative and original ideas; a well-reasoned plan with a way to assess success; the qualifications of the team; the resourcesA table of the two criteria and the five elements
NIH, Simplified Review FrameworkFactor 1, Importance of the Research (scored); Factor 2, Rigor and Feasibility (scored); Factor 3, Expertise and Resources (sufficient, or gaps)The three factors, and a check of the Specific Aims page
Heilmeier Catechism (DARPA)Eight plain questions: what you will do, how it is done today, what is new, who cares, the risks, the cost, the time, and the checks of successThe Heilmeier test

Rules and page limits change. Always use the current call and the funder's own pages. Links are on the Readings page.

Lab 1

Find your story

individualCode tabskills

Goal: set up the writing kit and the prompt log. Let Claude read your Word file. Then compare the story that you meant with the story that Claude reads in your draft. Correct the story yourself, in Word.

Install the writing kit

What and why

The skills and reviewers of the kit are about writing, not about one project. Thus you install them in your personal Claude folder, ~/.claude/. Then they work in all projects on this computer, for example your own project and the carbon example. A project can also have its own skills and agents in its .claude/ folder, as in Week 2. The skill files and agent files are plain text, and you can change them.

  1. Open a terminal. The Terminal pane of the Code tab is good.
  2. Check Python. On macOS, type python3 --version. In Windows PowerShell, type python --version. The kit needs Python 3.8 or later. If the command does not work, tell the instructor.
  3. Type these commands. They copy the skills and the reviewers from the kit that you downloaded at the start of class:

macOS

mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills ~/.claude/agents
cp -R ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/skills/. ~/.claude/skills/
cp ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/agents/*.md ~/.claude/agents/
ls ~/.claude/skills ~/.claude/agents

Windows PowerShell

Expand-Archive $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit.zip -DestinationPath $HOME\Downloads -Force
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force $HOME\.claude\skills, $HOME\.claude\agents | Out-Null
Copy-Item -Recurse -Force $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\skills\* $HOME\.claude\skills\
Copy-Item -Force $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\agents\*.md $HOME\.claude\agents\
Get-ChildItem $HOME\.claude\skills, $HOME\.claude\agents

You should see

Six skill folders: cite-check, flow-check, line-edit, proposal-check, story-check, and word-text. Four reviewer files: citation-reviewer.md, clarity-reviewer.md, methods-reviewer.md, and story-reviewer.md.

  1. Start a new session in your own project: + New session, environment Local, your project folder.
  2. Type /. Find story-check in the list.
  3. Open ~/.claude/agents/story-reviewer.md in a text editor. Find the tools: line and the skills: line.

If the skills or reviewers do not appear

Claude Code finds a new personal folder only in a new session. Start a new session. On a Mac, Finder hides .claude: press Cmd+Shift+. to see it. On Windows, use $HOME\.claude, also when OneDrive holds your Documents folder.

Start the prompt log

What and why

An instruction in a skill is a request: Claude usually obeys it. A hook is a guarantee. It is a small script that Claude Code runs at a set event, each time, whatever the model decides. This hook runs each time that you send a prompt (the UserPromptSubmit event). It adds the prompt to the file prompt-log.jsonl in your project. Start the hook before Claude reads your draft. Then you have a record of all that you asked, and your AI-use statement can be accurate.

  1. In the Terminal pane, go to your project folder.
  2. Copy the script with these commands:

macOS

mkdir -p .claude/hooks
cp ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/hooks/log_prompt.py .claude/hooks/

Windows PowerShell

New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force .claude\hooks | Out-Null
Copy-Item $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\hooks\log_prompt.py .claude\hooks\
  1. Send this prompt to Claude. It adds the hook to the settings of your project.

If the log stays empty
