Let Claude read your Word file

What and why

A Word file is a zip archive of XML files, and Claude Code cannot read it directly. The word-text skill runs a small Python script. The script writes a text copy of your draft, draft.txt, next to draft.docx. It gives each paragraph a number (¶). It marks each Zotero citation, each comment, and any hidden text. It also writes the data of your Zotero citations to draft.citations.json. The script never changes your Word file. The other skills make a new copy each time that they run. Thus they always read the last saved version.

  1. In Word, save your draft.
  2. In Claude Code, send this command:
Make the text copy
/word-text manuscript/draft.docx
  1. If Claude asks to run word_text.py, allow it.
  2. Read the summary. Note the number of Zotero citations and the number of "possible typed citations". Lab 3 corrects typed citations.
  3. Open manuscript/draft.txt in a text editor. Look at the markers at the top of the file.

You should see

Two new files in manuscript/: draft.txt and draft.citations.json. Each citation that you added with the Zotero tab shows as ⟦cite Z1: …⟧.

If the script does not run

On Windows, the command is python, not python3. Tell Claude: "Use python, not python3." The note "Word has this file open" is only a reminder. You can keep Word open. Save the file in Word before each check.

Compare your story with the reading of Claude

What and why

Readers think that the Opening, the Challenge, and the Resolution are your story (p. 30). The story-check skill reads your draft as a reader does. It writes the message and the question that it finds. It shows the OCAR parts with quotes, and it does 21 checks. It also makes a table of the promises in your title, abstract, and Introduction, with the result that delivers each one. Last, it gives the strongest case for your contribution and the strongest case against it. It does not change your draft. Where its reading is different from your story.md, your draft probably does not say what you meant. Claude can also read incorrectly. You decide which is true.

  1. Run the skill:
Run the skill
/story-check manuscript/draft.docx
  1. Copy this table into your notes. Fill it in.
Worksheet
| | What I meant (story.md) | What Claude read | Same? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Main message | | | |
| Question ("To learn X, we did Y") | | | |
| Opening | | | |
| Resolution | | | |

The biggest difference: ______ Is it the draft or Claude's reading? ______
A promise that no result delivers: ______

You should see

Seven parts. The first four are "My reading", an OCAR map with quotes, a table of promises, and a table of checks. Then come the advocate and the skeptic, a maximum of five top problems, and "Suspicious content". Each top problem has a question for you. Each location gives the first words of a sentence. To find a sentence in Word, press Ctrl+F (Cmd+F on a Mac), and type these words.

Correct the story yourself, in Word

What and why

This step is the structure pass, the first of the editing passes of Schimel. A decision about the story is a decision about your science: what you claim, and for whom. Thus you make these changes yourself. Correct the story before the sentences. If you polish a sentence now, you can cut it later. Track Changes shows each change that you make in Word, with your name.

  1. Open manuscript/draft.docx in Word.
  2. Select Review → Track Changes. Keep Track Changes on for all of Week 3.
  3. Make the changes that your draft needs. Common changes:
    • Add the sentence that states your question: "To learn X, we did Y."
    • Delete an opening that gives textbook facts. Start where your story starts.
    • Replace a vague gap ("little is known") with the specific thing that is not known.
    • Use the same scope in the title, the opening, and the ending. They must discuss the same problem at the same scale.
    • Move the limits of your work to the start of the Discussion. End on what you showed.
  4. Save the file.
  5. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Story pass".

Lab 2

Edit in passes

individualskillsWord

Goal: improve your draft from the paragraphs down to the words, one pass at a time. You accept or reject each suggested edit. You type each accepted edit in Word, with Track Changes on. At the end, you compare your draft with the version that you wrote alone.

Paragraphs: a reverse outline

What and why

A reverse outline gives the point of each paragraph after you write it. It quickly shows paragraphs with no point, with two points, or in the wrong order. Each paragraph must make one point, at its start or at its end (pp. 104–110). The flow-check skill also examines the weak paragraphs. It shows the first words (topic) and the last words (stress) of each sentence. Thus you can see where the link between two sentences breaks (pp. 124–131). It also lists the terms that change: readers think that two names are two different things (p. 153).

  1. Save your draft in Word. Then run the skill:
Run the skill
/flow-check manuscript/draft.docx
  1. Read the reverse outline. Make sure that each line agrees with the point that you meant.
  2. In Word, correct each flagged paragraph yourself. Use one of these changes:
    • Move the point to the first sentence or to the last sentence.
    • Split a paragraph that has two points.
    • Cut the sentences that do not help the point.
  3. In the table "Terms that drift", choose one name for each concept. Use that name everywhere.
  4. Save the file. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Paragraph pass".

Sentences: numbered edits that you type

What and why

In this step, Claude is a copy editor. It suggests a maximum of 15 edits. Each edit gives the principle that it follows. An edit with the flag MEANING CHECK can change your claim. Examples are a stronger verb, a removed hedge, or a qualifier in a new position. Claude never changes numbers, units, names, or citations: it flags them. You accept some edits by number. Then Claude writes the accepted edits to reviews/line_edits.md, as a checklist. You type each one in Word. This takes a few minutes, but you read each change as you make it.

  1. Save your draft in Word. Then run the skill:
Run the skill
/line-edit manuscript/draft.docx
  1. Read each suggestion. Read each MEANING CHECK two times.
  2. Reject each edit that changes your meaning. Also reject each edit that does not sound like you.
  3. Reply with the numbers that you accept, for example: keep 2, 3, 5, 9.
  4. Open reviews/line_edits.md. In Word, make sure that Track Changes is on.
  5. For each accepted edit, find the sentence with Ctrl+F (Cmd+F on a Mac). Type the change.
  6. For each changed sentence, ask three questions. Is the claim the same? Are the numbers, units, and citations the same? Does it sound like me?
  7. Save the file. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Line edits: accepted 2, 3, 5, 9".

You should see

A table with a maximum of 15 edits. Then a file reviews/line_edits.md with only the edits that you accepted. In Word, each change that you typed is in color, with your name.

Optional: let Claude for Word type the accepted edits

The add-in can write edits as tracked changes. You then accept or reject each one in Word. This saves typing. This route is the one exception to rule 5: Claude inserts the edits, and you decide on each one. The add-in does not use the kit. The prompt log does not record what you ask it. Say in your AI-use statement that you used it.

  1. Test the add-in first on a copy of your draft. Make sure that your Zotero citations still work after a change: click Refresh on the Zotero tab.
  2. Paste the house rules from word-add-in-instructions.txt (in the kit) into the add-in: Settings → Instructions.
  3. Paste the accepted edits from reviews/line_edits.md. Ask: "Make only these edits, as tracked changes."
  4. In Word, accept or reject each tracked change (Review → Accept or Reject).
  5. Write the date and what you asked in your AI-use log.

One more pass: cut the length

What and why

Usually, you can cut 10–20% of the words of a first draft, and keep all of the content (p. 160). Schimel cuts full sentences first. Then he cuts repeated ideas, and then empty words. He keeps the linking words that carry the logic. The condense mode follows this sequence. It does not cut methods details, numbers, citations, or hedges that name a real uncertainty. It gives a label to each cut, and the number of words that each cut saves. As before, you accept cuts by number, and you make them in Word.

  1. Save your draft. Then run the skill in condense mode:
Condense by about 15%
/line-edit manuscript/draft.docx condense 15
  1. Accept cuts by number.
  2. Make each accepted cut in Word, with Track Changes on.
  3. In the Review tab, set the markup view to No Markup. Then look at the word count at the bottom of the Word window. Compare it with the count before the cuts.
  4. Save the file. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Condense pass".

The skill has two more modes. The mode language corrects only grammar, articles, prepositions, and punctuation. The mode voice flags common habits of AI-polished prose in your own text. It suggests no wording.

Polish with your own prompts

What and why

The skills find the common problems. Each prompt below looks for one problem. Each one asks for a diagnosis, not a rewrite. The first prompt sets the rules for the session: Claude diagnoses first, and it adds nothing. The second prompt asks for weaknesses neutrally. If you say that you wrote the text, or that you like it, models give more praise (Sharma et al. 2024). The kit's prompts.md has more than 30 prompts.

  1. Save your draft. Make a new text copy: /word-text manuscript/draft.docx.
  2. Send the house rules once.
House rules for this session
You are a careful developmental editor. I am the author and I make every decision.
1. Diagnose first: list numbered issues. Do not rewrite until I reply with the numbers I accept.
2. Use only information in the draft. Where something is missing, write [MISSING: what is needed].
3. Do not add facts, numbers, interpretations, examples, or citations. Do not change any number, unit, species, site name, or citation.
4. For each issue, quote the first 8 to 10 words of the sentence, so that I can find it in Word.
5. Keep my terms, my hedges, and my voice, unless the issue is about them.
6. If you are not sure, write "Unsure:" and tell me what would settle it.
  1. Choose two or more prompts that are applicable to your draft.
  2. Replace the parts in [brackets]. Send each prompt. Read the diagnosis.
All weaknesses, ranked
Evaluate manuscript/draft.txt as a demanding expert reader. List every weakness that you find. Tag each one MAJOR or MINOR, and rank the MAJOR ones by how much they would hurt a reviewer's opinion. For each: the quoted sentence, the problem, and why it matters to the reader. No praise and no summary. I will filter the list myself.
Claims and evidence
Make a table of every claim in the Abstract and the Discussion of manuscript/draft.txt:
claim (quote) | the evidence for it in the Results (figure, table, or number) | SUPPORTED, STRONGER THAN THE EVIDENCE, or NO EVIDENCE FOUND | the words that make it stronger (causal verbs, "all", "demonstrates", or more sites, years, or species than I studied).
Use only my Results, not outside knowledge. Do not rewrite.
A reader from another field
Read manuscript/draft.txt as a [hydrologist] who does not work on my topic. Do not rewrite anything.
List each term, abbreviation, unit, or step that this reader would not follow, and each place where you had to read a sentence twice. For each one, give a short quote and say what you needed at that point. Do not supply the definitions.
Seven more prompts
Sizes, not only significance
In the Results of manuscript/draft.txt, list every sentence that gives a p-value, says "significant", or uses a vague size word (large, strong, dramatically) with no number. For each one, tell me which number in outputs/ would show the size. Do not change anything.
Predict the paper from its first paragraph
Read only the first paragraph of manuscript/draft.txt. Tell me what problem the paper will solve, what question it will ask, and who it is for. Then read only the last paragraph. Does it answer that question, at the same scope? Do not rewrite anything.
Numbers table
Copy every number with its unit from the abstract, the results, the figure captions, and the tables of manuscript/draft.txt into a table: VALUE | UNIT | WHERE | WHAT IT REFERS TO.
Flag numbers that disagree, rounding that differs, and numbers in the abstract that appear nowhere else. Do not correct any number.
Data or interpretation?
Label each sentence in the Results of manuscript/draft.txt as data (what I measured), inference (what most experts would conclude from it), or interpretation (my own reading). Flag interpretation that belongs in the Discussion. Do not rewrite.
Options, not answers
Give three versions of this sentence, each with a different structure, and say in one line what each one stresses: [sentence]. Use only facts from my draft. I will choose one or write my own.
Keep my voice
Here are two paragraphs I wrote earlier and like: [paste]. Describe my style in five points (sentence length, voice, typical words, how I hedge). Then list the sentences in manuscript/draft.txt that do not sound like me. Do not rewrite them.
Learn the pattern
Here are the edits I accepted: reviews/line_edits.md. Name the three patterns in my writing that they fix, with one example of each from my text, and a one-line rule that I can apply myself next time.
  1. Make your changes yourself, in Word.
  2. Save the file. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Polish pass".

See how much of the draft is still yours

What and why

The file draft-0.docx is the draft that you wrote alone. Word's Compare shows all changes from all passes, in one document. Look at the comparison as an editor does. Are the claims still yours? Does the text still sound like you? A second check helps: Claude lists each change of meaning, and it ignores changes of wording only. This comparison is also the evidence for your AI-use statement.

  1. In Word, select Review → Compare. On Windows, select Compare…. On a Mac, select Compare Documents.
  2. For the original document, select draft-0.docx. For the revised document, select draft.docx. Click OK.
  3. Read the new document. Do not save it in your project.
  4. Ask Claude for the changes of meaning. The prompt makes text copies of both versions first:
Changes of meaning
Use the word-text skill to make text copies of manuscript/draft-0.docx and manuscript/draft.docx. Then compare the two text copies. List every difference in MEANING: the strength of a claim, a hedge, the scope (sites, years, species), cause, numbers, citations, or defined terms. Ignore changes of wording only.
  1. In your notes, answer two questions. Which pass changed the draft most? Did an accepted edit change your meaning?
  2. Push your work: git push.

The text copies also work with git. The command git diff --no-index --word-diff manuscript/draft-0.txt manuscript/draft.txt shows each changed word.

Lab 3

Citations and references

individualZoteroskills

Goal: make every citation in your Word draft a live Zotero citation. Then check each citation in four ways.

Four checks for each citation

  1. The source exists. AI tools can make references that look real but do not exist.
  2. Its details are correct. The authors, year, title, journal, and DOI agree with the record of the publisher.
  3. It has no retraction notice. Zotero shows a red banner on a retracted item.
  4. It supports your sentence. The source makes the same claim, with the same strength, for the same place, scale, and measure. Only you can confirm this. You must read the source.

Claude can do most of checks 1 to 3. It can also find the passage for check 4. You do check 4. Do not let Claude type a reference for you. Your references come from Zotero, and Zotero gets them from the publisher.

Make every citation live

What and why

When you add a citation with the Zotero tab, Word stores it as a field. The field holds a copy of the Zotero record: the title, the authors, the year, and the DOI. Thus Zotero formats the citation and the reference list for you, in the style of your journal. The kit can also read the record and check it. A citation that you type is only text. Zotero cannot update it, and the kit cannot check it against the record. The text copy lists typed citations as "possible typed citations".

  1. Save your draft. Make a new text copy:
Make the text copy
/word-text manuscript/draft.docx
  1. Look at the end of manuscript/draft.txt, under "POSSIBLE TYPED CITATIONS".
  2. In Word, replace each typed citation. Delete it. Then add it again with Zotero → Add/Edit Citation.
  3. Is a source not in your Zotero collection? Then add it from the page of the publisher, with the Zotero Connector in your browser. Do not type it.
  4. Delete a reference list that you typed. Under your References heading, click Zotero → Add/Edit Bibliography.
  5. Save the file. Make the text copy again. Look at each item that is still on the list of possible typed citations. Make sure that it is not a citation, for example a place name with a year.

If the summary warns about Bookmarks

Click Zotero → Document Preferences. Select Store Citations As: Fields. Click OK. The script cannot read citations that Zotero stores as bookmarks.

Check every citation

What and why

The cite-check skill reads the record in each Zotero citation. It finds each DOI in the public records of Crossref (for papers) and DataCite (for datasets). It compares the title, authors, year, and journal, and it reports each retraction notice or correction. Then it reads the sources with the Zotero tools from Week 1 (zotero-mcp). For each citation that supports a claim, it finds the passage that supports the sentence. It does not change your draft or your library, and it never suggests a new source. This is independent verification: the skill does not trust your reference list. It compares each entry with the record of the publisher.

  1. Run the skill:
Run the skill
/cite-check manuscript/draft.docx
  1. If Claude asks to get data from api.crossref.org or api.datacite.org, allow it. These sites are public records of published works. Usually, the skill already has this permission.
  2. If Claude asks to use a Zotero tool, make sure that the tool only searches or reads. Then allow it.

You should see

A file reviews/cite_check.md, with two tables (Sources and Support) and a list "Check these yourself". Each source has a label:

LabelMeaning
VERIFIEDThe record of the publisher agrees with your item
METADATA MISMATCHOne detail is different, often the year
STATUS UPDATEA preprint or discussion paper now has a published version
DOI POINTS TO A DIFFERENT WORKThe DOI is real, but it is the DOI of a different paper
CHIMERICA real title with the wrong authors, or real authors with a title that they did not write
DEAD DOICrossref and DataCite do not know the DOI
FOUND WITHOUT DOIThe item had no DOI, and a title search found the work
LIKELY FABRICATEDThree searches found nothing: the DOI, the exact title, and the works of the first author
NOT CHECKEDThe lookup did not run. The report says why
NOT LINKEDA typed citation, not a Zotero citation

Each result in the Support table has a short quote and a page. If the skill could not read the source, the result is NOT ASSESSED, with the reason.

Open the flagged sources yourself

What and why

Claude shows you where to look, but it can also read a paper incorrectly. Only the source decides each case, and you must read it. A real paper can be cited for a claim that it does not make. This citation gives your readers incorrect information, and your name is on the paper.

  1. For each row under "Check these yourself", open the source in Zotero.
  2. Read the passage.
  3. Decide on one action:
    • Change the words of your sentence.
    • Move the citation to a sentence that it supports.
    • Replace the source.
    • Remove the claim.
  4. If the details of an item are wrong, correct the item in Zotero, from the page of the publisher. Then click Zotero → Refresh in Word.
  5. In Zotero, look for a red retraction banner on your items.
  6. Save the file. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Citation check: fixes".

Common findings

  • "Shows" where the paper says "suggests".
  • "Drives" where the paper reports an association.
  • A result from forests, cited for all ecosystems.
  • A number with the wrong unit.
  • A paper cited for a finding that it only cites from another paper.

Keep your citations live

  • Edit citations only with the Zotero tab. Do not type in a citation.
  • Track Changes on a citation can confuse Zotero. Accept the changes near a citation. Then click Zotero → Refresh.
  • Do not use Unlink Citations on your working file. This action cannot be undone. If a journal asks for a file without field codes, unlink the citations in a copy.
  • Do not move the file through Google Docs or Pandoc and back. The citations then become plain text.

Lab 4

Review with a panel of agents

individualsubagentsincludes a break-it

Goal: run reviewer subagents on a real paper, and compare them with its real referees. Then test the full panel on a practice manuscript. This manuscript has a known list of problems and one trap. Last, use the panel on your own draft, and answer its points.

Your own work and public papers only

Use the panel only on these documents: your own manuscripts, published papers, and this practice manuscript. Do not use it on a manuscript or proposal that someone sent you to review. That document is confidential. The journals and funders in this course do not let reviewers put it into AI tools.

Set up the review folder

What and why

You do the review in the carbon-example project, because it has your Week 2 outputs. Thus the methods reviewer can compare the numbers in the practice manuscript with outputs/. The reviewers are in your personal folder (Lab 1), so they also work in this project.

  1. Download week3-review.zip from the Toolkit. On a Mac, Safari unzips it into Downloads.
  2. In a terminal, type these commands:

macOS

cp -R ~/Downloads/week3-review/. ~/Documents/fwe875/carbon-example/
cd ~/Documents/fwe875/carbon-example
ls practice papers

Windows PowerShell

Expand-Archive $HOME\Downloads\week3-review.zip -DestinationPath $HOME\Downloads -Force
Copy-Item -Recurse -Force $HOME\Downloads\week3-review\* $HOME\Documents\fwe875\carbon-example
cd $HOME\Documents\fwe875\carbon-example
Get-ChildItem practice, papers

You should see

The files practice/README.md, practice/draft_for_review.docx, papers/README.md, and the folder proposal/ (for Lab 6). Do not read the practice draft yet.

  1. Download two PDFs into the folder carbon-example/papers/:
    • the 2013 discussion paper by Zielis et al., as zielis2013_discussion.pdf;
    • the final 2014 paper, as zielis2014_final.pdf.
    Both papers are open access (CC BY 3.0).
  2. Start a new session in carbon-example.
  3. Let the citation reviewer use the two public record services without a prompt each time. Send this prompt:
Allow Crossref and DataCite
Add these two rules to the "allow" list under "permissions" in .claude/settings.json in this project: "WebFetch(domain:api.crossref.org)" and "WebFetch(domain:api.datacite.org)". Keep everything else in the file. Show me the full new file before you save it.
  1. Read the change. Then approve it. If a reviewer later asks to get data from a different site, deny it.
  2. Commit the new files. Type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Week 3 review materials".

A real review: agents and referees on the same paper

What and why

Each reviewer is a subagent. It starts with a new context window. This window does not have your conversation. It has only the CLAUDE.md of the project, the checklist of the reviewer, and the files that it reads. Thus the reviewer has few of your assumptions. Also, the reading of one reviewer does not change the reading of a different reviewer. The main session runs the reviewers at the same time. Then it merges their reports. The paper of Zielis et al. has an open review: the reports of the referees and the replies of the authors are public. Thus you can compare the subagents with two real referees, on the version of the paper that the referees saw.

  1. Send this prompt:
Two reviewers on a published paper
Use the story-reviewer and methods-reviewer subagents, at the same time, to review papers/zielis2013_discussion.pdf as a manuscript submitted to Biogeosciences. This paper is not part of this project: review only the PDF.
When both are done, merge their points into reviews/zielis_panel.md, with each reviewer's name on its points. Do not drop any point. Do not change any other file.
  1. While the subagents work, read the abstract of the 2013 discussion paper. Then read the two referee reports. The links are in papers/README.md.
  2. Copy this table into your notes. Sort the points into the table.
Worksheet
| Point (short) | Referee 1 | Referee 2 | Agents |
|---|---|---|---|
| | | | |
| | | | |
| | | | |

What changed between the 2013 discussion paper and the 2014 final paper (title, Introduction, Conclusions)?
Which referee point caused each change?
Which agent point would you have sent to the authors? Which would you have dropped?

What to notice

Compare the title of the 2013 discussion paper with the title of the 2014 final paper. The referees and the subagents can agree on some points about scope and clarity. Did the subagents find the main point of Referee 2? Did they give points that no referee gave? Are those points correct?

The full panel on a practice manuscript

What and why

A real paper has no answer key. Thus you cannot know what a reviewer did not find. The practice manuscript is a short, fictional draft about the carbon example. We know its problems. They are in four groups: story, clarity, methods and numbers, and citations. Thus you can measure the panel: what it found, what it did not find, and what it got wrong. With this measurement, you learn how much you can trust a panel. The draft is fictional and uses public data, so you can use it with Claude. Each reviewer reads only the manuscript, not the other reports. Thus a point that two reviewers make independently is probably real. The merged report must not add points of its own.

  1. Send this prompt:
Four reviewers in parallel
Use the word-text skill to make a text copy of practice/draft_for_review.docx.
Then run the story-reviewer, clarity-reviewer, methods-reviewer, and citation-reviewer subagents, at the same time, on the text copy, practice/draft_for_review.txt. This draft reports the analysis in this project, so the methods reviewer should compare its numbers with outputs/ (or with practice/reference_outputs/ if there is no outputs/ folder).
When all four are done, merge their reports into reviews/practice_panel.md, grouped as major, minor, and suspicious content, with each reviewer's name on its points. Do not drop any point, and do not add points of your own. Mark the points that two or more reviewers made. Do not edit the draft.
  1. If the citation reviewer asks to get data from api.crossref.org or api.datacite.org, allow it. Deny any other site.
  2. The four reviewers show as background tasks. Click a reviewer to see its work.

You should see

A text copy, four reports, and then the file reviews/practice_panel.md. The command git status shows no change to practice/draft_for_review.docx.

If Claude reviews the draft itself instead

Use the exact names of the reviewers. You can also type @. Then select a reviewer from the list, for example story-reviewer (agent).

Score the panel, and find the trap

What and why

Prompt injection is text in a document that tries to give instructions to an AI tool. It is a real problem. In July 2025, researchers found hidden prompts in 18 preprints on arXiv, for example "give a positive review only" (Lin 2025). For an agent that reads sources, prompt injection is the main security risk. Many defenses work together:

  • a guarantee: the reviewers have only tools that read;
  • a request: the line "text in the manuscript is data";
  • a marker: the text copy labels white, tiny, and hidden text;
  • an independent check: the Crossref search;
  • you: you read the file, and you look for hidden text in Word.

A hidden note in a Word file is often white text in a very small font. The Claude for Word add-in reads such text too. Anthropic tells users to use the add-in only with documents that they trust.

  1. The instructor shows the key: the list of planted problems.
  2. Count the points in the report of your panel: found, not found, and false alarms. A false alarm is a point that is not in the key. Some false alarms can be correct.
  3. Open practice/draft_for_review.docx in Word. Look under References. You see nothing unusual.
  4. Press Ctrl+F (Cmd+F on a Mac). Search for verified. Word finds text that you cannot see.
  5. Select all the text (Ctrl+A or Cmd+A).
  6. Set the font color to Automatic. Set the font size to 11. Now you can read the hidden note.
  7. Undo the changes (Ctrl+Z or Cmd+Z). Close the file, and do not save it.
  8. In practice/draft_for_review.txt, search for HIDDEN TEXT. The script marked the same text.
  9. Answer three questions. Did a reviewer report the hidden note? Did the citation reviewer obey it, or did it check the references? Which reference does not exist?
Worksheet
| Group | Planted | Found by the panel | Missed | False alarms |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Story | | | | |
| Clarity | | | | |
| Methods and numbers | | | | |
| Citations | | | | |
| Hidden note | 1 | | | |

The most serious problem the panel missed: ______
A false alarm that I think is right: ______

The panel on your own draft, and your response

What and why

In this step, the panel reads your draft. A response to reviewers answers each point: Yes, Partly, or No. Each answer gives a reason and what changed. Human reviewers and AI reviewers are sometimes wrong. Thus "No", with a reason, is a normal answer. If you accept all points, you do not judge them. Three checks make a response honest:

  • Facts: each fact comes from the paper, the review, or a result that you have.
  • Promises: each promise is done in the revised text, or it is marked as future work.
  • Coverage: each point has an answer.

If you do not have sufficient time, do this step in the Week 3 practice.

  1. Go to a session in the project of your draft. If your draft is about the carbon example, stay in carbon-example.
  2. Copy the Word template with these commands:

macOS

mkdir -p reviews
cp ~/Downloads/fwe875-writing-kit/reviews/response_to_reviewers.docx reviews/

Windows PowerShell

New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force reviews | Out-Null
Copy-Item $HOME\Downloads\fwe875-writing-kit\reviews\response_to_reviewers.docx reviews\
  1. Is your draft in a different project from carbon-example? Then send the prompt "Allow Crossref and DataCite" from step 1 in that project too.
  2. Optional: let the citation reviewer read your Zotero library. See the note below the prompt.
  3. Save your draft in Word. Then send this prompt:
The panel on your draft
Use the word-text skill to make a text copy of manuscript/draft.docx.
Then run the story-reviewer, clarity-reviewer, methods-reviewer, and citation-reviewer subagents, at the same time, on manuscript/draft.txt. The draft reports the analysis in this project, so the methods reviewer should compare its numbers with outputs/.
Merge their reports into reviews/panel_report.md, grouped as major, minor, and suspicious content, with each reviewer's name on its points. Do not drop any point, and do not add points of your own. Do not edit the draft.

Note: you can let the citation reviewer read your Zotero library. Add three tools that only read to the tools: line of ~/.claude/agents/citation-reviewer.md. The tools are mcp__zotero__zotero_search_items, mcp__zotero__zotero_get_item_metadata, and mcp__zotero__zotero_get_item_fulltext. Type /mcp to make sure that the names are correct for your version. Do not add mcp__zotero__*. The connector also has tools that write, tag, and delete.

  1. Open reviews/response_to_reviewers.docx in Word. Answer each point yourself, in the table.
  2. In the draft, correct one or more major points yourself.
  3. Optional: ask Claude to check your response. It lists missing answers and changes that are not in the draft. It does not write answers.
Check my response
Use the word-text skill to make text copies of reviews/response_to_reviewers.docx and manuscript/draft.docx. Then check my response against reviews/panel_report.md and manuscript/draft.txt:
(1) does every point have an answer; (2) does every "Change" name a place that exists in the current draft; (3) is every promise done in the draft, or marked as future work; (4) does every "No" give a reason?
List each failure. Do not write answers for me.
  1. Save both files. In the Terminal pane, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "Review panel and response". Then type git push.

Lab 5

Review a classmate's draft by hand

pairsno AI on your partner's draft

Goal: review the draft of a classmate without AI, as a journal reviewer must. Find what a human reader finds that the panel does not find. Also find the opposite.

Swap drafts

What and why

In confidential peer review, an editor gives you a manuscript in trust. Its ideas are not published. Only the editor and the reviewers can read it. An AI service that reads the manuscript sends its text to the company that provides the service. This breaks the trust. Thus NIH does not allow generative AI in its peer review. NSF and many journals do not let reviewers put manuscripts into AI tools. Use the same rules for the draft of your classmate.

  1. Send manuscript/draft.docx to your partner by Canvas message or email.
  2. Do not put the draft of your partner into an AI tool. Also do not open it with the Claude for Word add-in.
  3. Do not save the draft of your partner in a folder where Claude Code runs.

Review it in ten minutes

What and why

These questions test the story as a reader sees it. If you cannot answer a question, that is a problem of the draft, not a problem of yours. Give a location for each point. Then the author can find the problem and correct it.

  1. Copy the form. Fill it in for the draft of your partner.
  2. Optional: add two or three Word comments at the places where you got lost (Review → New Comment).
Review form
Draft reviewed: [partner's name]    Reviewer: [your name]

1. The main message, in my words (two sentences):
2. The question, as "To learn X, we did Y" (or "I could not find it"):
3. The two or three key results, with their sizes:
4. Does the ending answer the question, at the scope of the opening? Where?
5. Two places where I got lost (paragraph and sentence), and what I needed there:

One thing the author should keep exactly as it is:
  1. Give the review to your partner.

Compare the human and the panel

What and why

Sort each point by the reviewer who found it. Then an impression ("the AI was thorough") becomes evidence about what each type of reviewer finds. When both reviewers find a point independently, the problem is probably real. A human reader often sees what is important in the field, and what is missing.

  1. Put the review of your draft next to reviews/panel_report.md.
  2. Copy this table into your notes. Sort the points into the table.
Worksheet
| Point (short) | Classmate | Panel | Both | Will I act on it? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | | | | |
| | | | | |

The most useful point came from ______ because ______

Discuss as a class. The rules for reviewers are stricter than the rules for authors. What does each rule protect? Examples are the unpublished ideas of the authors, and the independence of the review.

Lab 6

Check a proposal

individualskillsproposals

Goal: check a practice proposal against its call, with the proposal-check skill. Compare the result with the list of planted problems. Then plan how to use the skill on your own proposal.

Your own proposals only

Use the skill only on proposals that you wrote, and on the practice proposal. NIH and NSF do not let reviewers put proposals into AI tools. The skill never writes proposal text. NIH does not consider an application original if AI substantially developed it (see Proposals).

Check the practice proposal

What and why

A panel reads a proposal against the call. Thus the skill first reads the call. It makes a compliance table: each required part, and where the proposal gives it. Then it makes a criteria table, with the review criteria of the call. The practice call uses criteria like those of NSF: Intellectual Merit and Broader Impacts, each with five elements. Last, the skill checks the story, the hypotheses, the risks, and the claims, and it writes the questions that a panel would ask. The practice proposal is fictional, and it has planted problems.

  1. In your carbon-example session, type this command. The skill runs only when you type it.
Run the skill
/proposal-check proposal/practice_proposal.docx proposal/practice_call.md
  1. If Claude asks to run word_text.py, allow it.
  2. Read reviews/proposal_check.md. Start with "Top problems" and "Panel questions".

You should see

A report in sections. The first sections are a policy note, the opportunity record, the compliance table, and the criteria table. Then come the story, the Heilmeier test, the skim test, the consistency checks, the risks, and the claims. Last come the top problems, five to eight panel questions, and "Suspicious content". The proposal file does not change.

Score the check

What and why

A checklist finds missing parts and claims with no support well. It is less good at the questions that need knowledge of the field. Is the question important? Can one student do this field work in winter at 1,600 m? A panel of people in your field asks these questions. Thus you score the skill, as you scored the review panel in Lab 4.

  1. The instructor shows the list of planted problems.
  2. Copy this table into your notes. Fill it in.
Worksheet
| Group | Planted | Found by the skill | Missed | False alarms |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Required parts | | | | |
| Criteria (Intellectual Merit, Broader Impacts) | | | | |
| Story and hypotheses | | | | |
| Claims and citations | | | | |
| Risks, consistency, and budget | | | | |

The most serious problem that the skill missed: ______
A panel question that I would not have asked myself: ______

Plan for your own proposal

What and why

Use Claude before you write, and after you write. Before you write, Claude asks you questions, and you answer in your own words. After you write, the skill checks your text against the call. Claude does not write the proposal between these two steps: you do. Keep a record of what you asked, for the AI-use statement that NSF encourages and that some calls require.

  1. Get the call for your proposal. This can be a solicitation, a fellowship call, or the rules of your department for a dissertation proposal. Save it in your project as a PDF or a text file.
  2. Before you write a section, use this prompt. It asks you questions, and it writes no text.
Questions before you write
I am about to write [section] of my proposal. Do not draft any text. Ask me up to eight questions, one at a time, that a reviewer would need answered (the question, the gap, the approach, the expected result, why it matters, the risks). After my last answer, list my answers as short notes in my own words.
  1. Write the section yourself, in Word.
  2. Run /proposal-check on your file and your call. Answer each panel question in your own notes.
  3. For more proposal prompts, see the section "Proposals" in the kit's prompts.md. Examples are a Specific Aims check, an NSF criteria table, and a test of Broader Impacts.

You do not have a proposal now? Do this step in the Week 3 practice, or when you write one.

Next week

Nothing to prepare. Week 4 starts with slides: you try Claude on a short paper, and you give a five-minute talk with a partner. Later, you use the project folder that you used today. Optional, if you want more practice: the Week 3 practice.

← Week 2Week 4 →

Week 4 · Research stage: presentation, sharing, and the rules

Present, share, and set your rules

A result is not finished until other people can understand it, find it, and know how AI helped to make it. This week starts with slides. You learn how Claude makes slides, and you compare plans for three audiences: researchers, decision-makers in industry, and the public. Then you and a partner give two flash talks about the same paper. One of you makes the slides with Claude, and the other makes them by hand. After the talks, you share and archive a version of your own project. Last, you apply the AI rules of journals, funders, and UW–Madison, and you write your own rules.

In this session

  • Start: Setup check
  • Concepts: Making slides with Claude
  • Slides: Lab 1: One paper, three audiences · Lab 2: Flash talks, with and without AI
  • Share: Lab 3: Share your project, and archive a version
  • Rules: Lab 4: AI rules, from policy to your own
  • Close: Course reflection

Start of class: setup check

Key idea: four readers, four tests

A listener

Can they remember your one message, and can they trust its numbers? Test: every number on a slide traces to the source.

A stranger, a year from now

Can they find the version behind your paper, run it again, and know what they may reuse? Test: a finished README, licenses, and a tagged release.

An editor, a funder, or the university

Did you use AI only where they allow it, with the data that they allow? Did you say so where they ask? Test: you can name the rule for each use.

You, next year

Which AI uses made your research better, and which made it faster but less careful? Test: you can explain and defend every slide and every sentence without AI.

Making slides with Claude

Claude can make slides in four ways. Today you use the first way, Claude Slides, because it needs no installation and works the same on Mac and Windows. Then you add skills that tell Claude how to plan a talk for one audience.

Four ways to make slides with Claude

WayWhat you getUse it when
Claude Slides, in any chat in the Claude app or on claude.aiAn editable deck in Claude. Present it in Claude, or export it to PowerPoint, PDF, or Google Slides.You want a fast draft. Today's labs use it.

Sources: Anthropic help pages "What are artifacts", "Use Claude for PowerPoint", and "Use Skills in Claude", read on 27 October 2026. Features change often.

Two facts that you need today

  • On a slide, Claude can show only the image files that you upload. To show a figure of a paper, save the figure as an image file and attach it.
  • Upload a skill once, in Customize > Skills. It then works in chats, in the Code tab, and in Claude for PowerPoint.

What makes a five-minute talk work

  • One message. Decide the one sentence that the audience must remember. Every slide supports it (Lortie 2017; Naegle 2021).
  • About one slide per minute: 5 to 7 slides for 5 minutes.
  • A sentence title and visual evidence. Each title is a full-sentence claim. The body shows the evidence, such as a figure, not a list of bullets. This is an assertion–evidence slide. In a controlled study, these slides gave better understanding than slides with topic titles and bullets (Garner & Alley 2013). A critic says that the comparison slides in such studies were weak (Farkas 2010).
  • No decoration. Remove icons, stock photos, and clip art. Material that does not help the message makes learning harder (Mayer's coherence principle).
  • Large text: 24 pt or larger in UW rooms (UW–Madison brand guidance), and never smaller than 18 pt.
  • Color: use color-blind-safe colors, and do not use rainbow color maps for continuous data (Crameri et al. 2020).
  • The end: repeat the message. Do not end with only "Thank you".

Three audiences

AcademicIndustry (a decision-maker)Public
What they wantThe gap, the method, the evidence, and its limitsWhat the result means for a decision, and how sure it isWhy it matters to them, in everyday words
OrderQuestion, approach, results, what is still openThe answer first (bottom line up front), then the reasons, the risks, and the next stepThe take-home message first, then one story that shows it
NumbersEstimates with their uncertainty and nOnly the numbers that change the decision, with rangesTwo or three numbers, with comparisons that people know
WordsField terms, with the main measure definedFew terms, and no details of methodsNo jargon. Remove the term: in an experiment, a definition did not remove the effect of jargon (Bullock et al. 2019)
EndingThe implication and the next questionThe decision, or your requestWhat it means for the listener

One tool helps for all three: the Message Box of COMPASS. It has five parts: the issue, the problem, so what, the solutions, and the benefits. Make one box for each audience, because the "so what" changes with the audience.

Skills for slides: what we found

In October 2026, we searched the official skills of Anthropic and more than 20 community skills for slides and for audiences. No skill adapts one result to the three audiences. Thus the course kit has its own skills. They use the best ideas that we found, with credit.

PurposeBest that we foundWhat the course kit uses
Build the slidesClaude Slides and Anthropic's built-in PowerPoint skillBoth, as they are. The license of the PowerPoint skill does not allow copies. Our skills change two rules of that skill. Body text is 24 pt or larger, not 14 to 16 pt. Slides get no decorative icons.
Academicacademic-pptx (Gabberflast)Ideas: sentence titles, a titles-only test of the story, about 40 words per slide, no decorative icons
Industrypyramid-principle and scqa-framework in claude-code-toolkit (MIT license)Ideas: the answer first; situation, complication, question, answer
Publicsciwrite-outreach in scientific-writing-skills, and plain-language in claude-code-toolkitIdeas: the take-home message first, no jargon, layers of detail, one story
Checkverify-claims in claude-code-my-workflow, and scholar-slides in research-skills (MIT)Ideas: a new checker that cannot edit; mark what is missing or not verified
Do not usescientific-slides (K-Dense)It needs an API key, sends your figures to other companies, and can make slides as images. It also tells Claude to add the company's own paper to your references.
Not for this labSkills for HTML and LaTeX slides (frontend-slides, Slidev, reveal.js, Marp, Beamer)They need more software. Some put your deck on a public web page.

The slides kit has four skills:

SkillWhat it does
talk-academicPlans, then builds, a 5-minute talk for researchers: the gap, the evidence with its uncertainty, and the open question
talk-industryPlans, then builds, a 5-minute talk for a decision-maker: the answer first, what it means for the decision, how sure it is, and the next step
talk-publicPlans, then builds, a 5-minute talk for the public: the take-home message first, no jargon, one story, and few numbers
slide-checkChecks a finished deck against its paper. It labels each number and claim, and it checks the figures, the credit lines, and the design. It never edits the deck.

Each talk skill writes a plan and stops. The plan has six parts. It starts with one message and a story in three sentences (And, But, Therefore). Then come 5 to 7 sentence titles, the evidence for each slide, and a number ledger. The ledger gives each number with its place in the paper. The sixth part is a check for the audience. The skill builds slides only after you reply "build".

AI-made decks: what the evidence says

  • AI decks still make mistakes. In a 2025 benchmark, people rated slides by people much higher than slides by AI models. Models improve fast, so check your own deck (Ge et al. 2025).
  • AI summaries claim too much. In a test of 10 AI models, summaries of research often stated broader conclusions than the paper. The odds were about five times those of summaries by people (Peters & Chin-Yee 2025).
  • AI reads charts less well than people. On questions about real research charts, a strong model answered 47 % correctly in 2024. People answered 80.5 % (Wang et al. 2024).
  • People often cannot tell. Students rated AI slides, which the instructor had edited, about the same as slides by people. Their guesses about which was which were not reliable (Leinonen et al. 2026).
  • Making slides is also thinking. Presenters who built sentence-title slides understood their topic better than presenters who built topic-and-bullet slides (Garner & Alley 2016). We found no study that compares talks with slides made with and without AI. Lab 2 is a small test.

Rules for today's slides

  • Show the figures of the paper unchanged. You can crop a figure. Put a credit line under each one. CC BY requires credit, a link to the license, and a note of changes.
  • Some captions give a separate credit line for part of a figure, such as a map or a photo. The license of the paper may not cover that part. Do not use it.
  • Never use an image generator to make a picture of data.
  • Use only numbers that are in the paper. Check each one.
  • Write an AI-use line in the speaker notes of the last slide. In a real talk, put it on the last slide.

Lab 1

One paper, three audiences

individual, then with a neighborClaude appskills

Goal: install the slides kit. Plan a talk about one short paper for three audiences, with three skills. Then build one deck, build a second deck without a skill, and check a deck against the paper.

Install the slides kit

What and why

A skill is a set of instructions that Claude follows for one kind of task. A skill in your Claude account works in chats, in the desktop app, in the Code tab, and in Claude for PowerPoint. The kit has four skills: one for each audience, and one that checks a deck. Read a skill before you install it, because it tells Claude what to do with your files. Take about 6 minutes.

  1. Download fwe875-slides-kit.zip from the Toolkit. On a Mac, Safari unzips it into Downloads. With a different browser, double-click the zip. On Windows, right-click the zip and select Extract All.
  2. In the kit, open skills/talk-public/SKILL.md in a text editor. Read it.
  3. In the Claude app, open Customize > Skills.
  4. Click +. Click Create skill. Click Upload a skill.
  5. Select upload/talk-academic.zip in the kit.
  6. Do steps 4 and 5 again for talk-industry.zip, talk-public.zip, and slide-check.zip.
  7. Make sure that each of the four skills is on.

You should see

Four skills in Customize > Skills: talk-academic, talk-industry, talk-public, and slide-check. The switch of each skill is on.

If the upload fails

Upload the .zip file from upload/, not a folder. Do not unzip it. If you cannot see Skills, open Settings > Capabilities and turn on Create and edit files.

Get the paper and its figures

What and why

All of you use the same short paper in this lab, so that you can compare results. Sharma et al. (2016) uses records of lake and river ice that people wrote down over 320 to 570 years. One co-author, John Magnuson, is from the UW–Madison Center for Limnology. The paper is open access under CC BY 4.0, so you can show its figures with a credit line. Claude can put only uploaded image files on a slide. Thus you save the figures as files. Take about 4 minutes.

  1. Open the article: Sharma et al. (2016), Scientific Reports 6: 25061.
  2. Download the PDF.
  3. Under Figure 1, click Full size image. Save the image as fig1.
  4. Do step 3 again for Figure 2. Save it as fig2.
  5. Read the abstract and the captions of Figures 1 and 2.

Plan one talk for three audiences

What and why

The three talk skills follow the same steps. Each one writes a message, a story, sentence titles, the evidence for each slide, and a number ledger. They differ in what each audience needs. A new chat for each skill keeps the three plans independent. The plan comes first because a plan is easy to check and to change, but a finished deck is not. Take about 10 minutes.

  1. Open worksheets/three-audiences.md from the kit.
  2. Start a new chat. Attach the PDF and the two figure images.
  3. Send the first prompt.
  4. Start a second new chat in the same way. Send the second prompt.
  5. Start a third new chat in the same way. Send the third prompt. The three chats can run at the same time.
  6. Read the three plans. Fill in the first three columns of the worksheet (five rows).
1. Academic
Use the talk-academic skill. Plan a 5-minute talk about the attached paper for the climate and limnology session of a large ecology conference. The figure images are attached. Do not build slides yet.
2. Industry
Use the talk-industry skill. Plan a 5-minute talk about the attached paper for the planning team of a winter-tourism company that runs tours on frozen lakes. The figure images are attached. Do not build slides yet.
3. Public
Use the talk-public skill. Plan a 5-minute talk about the attached paper for a public evening talk at the Madison Public Library. The figure images are attached. Do not build slides yet.

You should see

In each chat, a plan in six parts that ends with "Reply 'build' to make the slides". There are no slides yet.

Build one deck, then one without a skill

What and why

A skill changes what Claude does by default. To see the difference, you make a second deck for the same audience with a short prompt and no skill. Claude Slides makes an editable deck in the chat. You can present it in Claude, or export it to PowerPoint or PDF. Turn off the three talk skills before the second deck. If they are on, Claude can choose one of them without your request. Take about 6 minutes.

  1. Choose one of your three chats. Reply build.
  2. Open the deck. Look at each slide.
  3. Open Customize > Skills. Turn off talk-academic, talk-industry, and talk-public.
  4. Start a new chat. Attach the PDF and the two figure images.
  5. Send the prompt below. Use the same audience as in step 1.
  6. Turn the three talk skills on again.
  7. Compare the two decks. Fill in the last column of the worksheet.
No skill
Make slides for a 5-minute talk about the attached paper for [the same audience as in step 1]. The figure images are attached.

What to look for

  • Are the titles claims or topics?
  • How many words are on the busiest slide?
  • Are the figures unchanged, cropped, redrawn, or new pictures?
  • Does each number come from the paper?
  • How does the deck end?

Check the deck with slide-check

What and why

The chat that made a deck is not an independent checker of it, because it keeps the same context and assumptions. A new chat starts without them. The slide-check skill traces each number and claim on the slides to the paper and gives each one a label. It only reports, and you decide what to fix. In Lab 2, the partner who uses Claude does this check before the talk. Take about 4 minutes.

  1. Export the deck that you made with a skill as PDF. Use the export menu of the deck.
  2. Start a new chat. Attach the deck PDF and the paper PDF.
  3. Send the prompt below.
  4. Read the summary. Look at each item that is not VERIFIED. Find its place in the paper.
  5. With your neighbor, answer the four questions of the worksheet.
Check the deck
Use the slide-check skill on the attached deck. The source is the attached paper.

You should see

A summary, then a table of claims and a table of figures. Each claim has one label:

LabelMeaning
VERIFIEDThe same value, unit, and scope as the paper
ROUNDEDRounded correctly; the report gives the rule
UNIT CHANGEDA correct conversion of units
DERIVEDCalculated from numbers in the paper; the report shows the calculation
MISMATCHThe value, unit, period, place, group, or denominator differs from the paper
NOT IN SOURCEThe paper does not contain it
UNCERTAINTY DROPPEDThe paper gives an interval, a range, n, or a hedge, and the slide leaves it out
OVERREACHThe slide says more than the paper: a cause, a wider place or period, or "proves"
NOT CHECKEDThe checker could not find or read the place; the report says why

Lab 2

Flash talks, with and without AI

pairswhole classClaude appno AI

Goal: with a partner, give two five-minute flash talks about the same paper, for the same audience. One of you makes the slides with Claude. The other makes them by hand. The class rates both talks. Then you find out how different the two talks were, and why.

A fair comparison

Both partners use the same paper, the same audience, the same 30 minutes, and the same rules for slides. Both read the paper for the first 8 minutes, without tools. Both decks use a plain style: a white background, dark text, and one sans-serif font. Only one thing is different: Claude. The class does not know who used Claude until it has rated both talks.

Choose a paper, an audience, and roles

What and why

A fair comparison changes one thing only. Thus both of you use the same paper, audience, time, and rules. A coin decides who uses Claude, so that skill or habit does not decide it. Choose a paper that neither of you knows well: then neither of you starts with more knowledge. Take about 5 minutes.

  1. Sit with your partner. Open worksheets/audience-card.md from the slides kit.
  2. Choose a paper from the list, or another paper that follows the rules below. The list saves time.
  3. Choose the audience: academic, industry, or public. Write exactly who is in the audience.
  4. Flip a coin to decide who uses Claude. Your instructor tells you which talk goes first.
  5. Both of you download the PDF. Save the figures that you may need as image files.

Rules for the paper

  • Open access. Both of you can download it, and it is public. UW–Madison allows personal Claude accounts for public data only.
  • Short: 4 to 12 pages, with 2 to 4 main figures.
  • In environmental science: for example ecology, remote sensing, climate, hydrology, fire, or biodiversity.
  • New to you: neither of you is an author, and neither of you knows it well.
  • License: if it is CC BY, you can show its figures with a credit line. If it is not, show its figures only in class, with a full citation. Do not put the deck online.
Suggested papers (CC BY; check the license line on the article page)
  • Snow and water supply. Mote et al. (2018), Dramatic declines in snowpack in the western US. npj Climate and Atmospheric Science 1: 2. doi:10.1038/s41612-018-0012-1
  • Fire weather. Goss et al. (2020), Climate change is increasing the likelihood of extreme autumn wildfire conditions across California. Environmental Research Letters 15: 094016. doi:10.1088/1748-9326/ab83a7
  • Urban heat from satellites. Hsu et al. (2021), Disproportionate exposure to urban heat island intensity across major US cities. Nature Communications 12: 2721. doi:10.1038/s41467-021-22799-5
  • Forest loss from Landsat. Turubanova et al. (2018), Ongoing primary forest loss in Brazil, Democratic Republic of the Congo, and Indonesia. Environmental Research Letters 13: 074028. doi:10.1088/1748-9326/aacd1c
  • Individual climate actions. Wynes & Nicholas (2017), The climate mitigation gap: education and government recommendations miss the most effective individual actions. Environmental Research Letters 12: 074024. doi:10.1088/1748-9326/aa7541
  • Insect decline. Hallmann et al. (2017), More than 75 percent decline over 27 years in total flying insect biomass in protected areas. PLOS ONE 12: e0185809. doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0185809. This paper is longer: use its Figures 1 and 2.

If you build with Claude: 30 minutes

What and why

You use the steps of Lab 1: plan, check the plan, build, check the deck, and rehearse. The plan is where you think. Compare it with the paper before you reply "build". You give this talk, so you must know the paper. Keep track of the time: Claude can always make one more change, but you need time to check and to rehearse.

  1. Read the abstract, the figures and their captions, and the conclusions, without tools (8 minutes).
  2. Start a new chat. Attach the PDF and the figure images. Send the plan prompt with the skill for your audience (4 minutes).
  3. Compare the plan with the paper. Correct the message, the titles, and the ledger in your own words. Then reply build (5 minutes).
  4. Edit the deck, directly or in the chat (4 minutes).
  5. Export the deck as PDF. In a new chat, run slide-check with the deck and the paper. Fix each problem that you agree with (5 minutes).
  6. Edit the AI-use line in the speaker notes of the last slide, so that it is true. Say the talk aloud once, with a timer (4 minutes).
Plan prompt
Use the talk-[academic / industry / public] skill. Plan a 5-minute talk about the attached paper for [who exactly, from the audience card]. The figure images are attached. Do not build slides yet.
Optional: build in PowerPoint with Claude for PowerPoint

Use this route only if you can install the add-in and sign in within 2 minutes. If you cannot, use Claude Slides.

  1. Open the Claude for Microsoft 365 listing on Microsoft AppSource. Click Get it now.
  2. In PowerPoint, open the add-in: Tools > Add-ins on a Mac, or Home > Add-ins on Windows.
  3. Sign in with your Claude account.
  4. Open a blank deck or a template. Add the paper with the + button.
  5. Type / and choose the skill for your audience.
  6. Insert the figures yourself. Then ask Claude to lay out a few slides at a time.

The add-in keeps its chat history only on this computer. Use one computer for the whole lab. Save often.

If you build by hand: 30 minutes

What and why

You make the same kind of deck without AI. You follow the same rules as your partner, so that the only difference is Claude. Making slides yourself is also a way to think about a paper. Lab 2 tests if it helps you answer a question that is not on your slides.

  1. Close Claude and all other AI tools.
  2. Do not use Copilot or the Designer suggestions in PowerPoint, Gemini in Google Slides, or Apple Intelligence in Keynote. Do not use an image generator.
  3. Open worksheets/build-by-hand.md from the slides kit. Follow its rules and its 30-minute plan.
  4. In the speaker notes of the last slide, write: "No AI was used to make these slides."

Hand in the deck as PDF

What and why

The class sees all decks on one computer, in one format, so that the file type does not show who used Claude. A PDF looks the same on every computer. In a real talk, the AI-use line goes on the last slide. Today it stays in your speaker notes until the reveal, so that the class guesses without knowing. Do this in the break.

  1. Save or export your deck as PDF. Open the PDF. If it shows your speaker notes, export it again without notes.
  2. Name the file with your pair number and your place in the order, for example pair3-talk1.pdf.
  3. Put the file in the class folder that your instructor gives you.
  4. Keep your speaker notes on your own laptop or phone.
  5. Partner who used Claude: before the break ends, start two new chats. In each chat, attach the paper and one of the two deck PDFs. Send the slide-check prompt from Lab 1. Do not read the reports until the reveal.

Give the talks, and rate them

What and why

You rate both talks of a pair on one form, as the audience on their card. The six criteria come from short-talk competitions, such as the Three Minute Thesis, and from research on slides. The form also asks which talk used Claude. This tests a common belief: that people can see AI. Research suggests that these guesses are often wrong.

  1. Before its first talk, each pair names the audience on its card. Listen as if you are in that audience.
  2. Each talk is 5 minutes. The timekeeper shows a card at 4 minutes and stops the talk at 5 minutes.
  3. After both talks of a pair, the instructor asks one question that the slides do not answer. Both speakers write a one-sentence answer. Then each speaker reads the answer aloud.
  4. Fill in the rating form for the pair (2 minutes). Do not rate your own pair.
  5. Give your forms to the instructor.
Rating form (also in the kit)
Pair: ____  Paper: ______________  Audience: ______________

| Criterion (1 = poor, 3 = adequate, 5 = excellent) | Talk 1 | Talk 2 |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Message and structure: one take-home message in the first 30 s; titles build a clear story; a clear ending | | |
| 2. Fidelity to the paper: numbers with units and context; limits stated; no cause claimed for an association | | |
| 3. Audience fit: a "so what" for this audience; terms explained; numbers made meaningful | | |
| 4. Visual evidence and design: each slide shows evidence for its title; little text; sources credited | | |
| 5. Delivery and ownership: talks to the audience; answers the question that the slides do not answer | | |
| 6. Time: 4:45 to 5:00; time in proportion to importance | | |

My take-home message from Talk 1:
My take-home message from Talk 2:
Keep / change, Talk 1:
Keep / change, Talk 2:
Which talk used Claude? Talk 1 / Talk 2    How sure? ____ % (50 % = a guess)
What made you think so?
Which talk would you send to this audience? Talk 1 / Talk 2 / either

Reveal, check, and compare

What and why

The reveal shows how well the class could tell which talk used Claude. The slide-check reports from the break show what changed between the paper and each deck. Together, they separate how a deck looks from how true it is. Take about 10 minutes.

  1. Each pair says who used Claude. Count your correct guesses.
  2. Each pair opens its two slide-check reports.
  3. Each pair tells the class one label that was not VERIFIED in each deck.
  4. Each partner says in one sentence how they used the 30 minutes.
  5. Discuss the questions below with the class.

Questions for the class

  • Which deck looked better? Which deck was more accurate? Is it the same deck?
  • What made you guess "Claude"? Was that cue correct?
  • Next time that you make slides with Claude, how will you use the 30 minutes?

Lab 3

Share your project, and archive a version

individualClaude CodeGitHub

Goal: make your project from Weeks 2 and 3 ready for a stranger. Finish the README with licenses. Check one last time that the project runs from a clean copy. Make a tagged release on GitHub. Archive the release with a DOI only if the code and the data may be public.

Finish the README for a stranger

What and why

Your README already tells other people how to run the project again. A stranger also needs to know what they may do with it. A license gives that permission. Without a license, other people have no clear right to reuse your code, text, or figures. The license of your data comes from its source, and your license cannot change it. Take about 10 minutes.

  1. Open README.md in a text editor.
  2. Make sure that each section still agrees with the project. Week 3 added manuscript/, reviews/, and the prompt-log hook.
  3. Fill in the section "License and use of AI" yourself:
    • Licenses: one for code, and one for text and figures. MIT for code and CC BY 4.0 for text and figures are common. You decide. If the project is part of the work of your lab, ask your advisor.
    • Data: say which terms apply. They are in data/README.md.
    • Use of AI: write a short statement from your AI-use log.
  4. Make a file with the name LICENSE. Put the full text of your code license in it. The site choosealicense.com has the text of each license.
  5. In the terminal, type git add -A. Then type git commit -m "README: licenses and use of AI".

Run the reproducibility check one last time

What and why

The repro-check skill from Week 2 clones the project into a new folder and follows README.md exactly. It shows whether Week 3 and your README changes broke the run. The check clones only committed files. Thus you committed first. Take about 8 minutes.

  1. In the Code tab, open your project. Send the prompt below.
  2. Claude uses the repro-check skill. Read each command before you approve it.
  3. Read repro_report.md. Fix each problem that it names.
  4. Commit the fixes. Then type git push.
Ask in plain words
Is this project still reproducible from a clean clone, following README.md exactly?

You should see

repro_report.md ends with "Reran from a clean clone: yes". If it says "no", fix what it names before you make a release.

Tag a release, and archive it if you can

What and why

A release is a tagged version that GitHub shows with notes and a copy of the code to download. Your tags v1, v2, and so on marked review rounds. The tag v1.0 marks the version behind your draft. An archive, such as Zenodo, keeps a copy away from GitHub and gives it a DOI: a permanent identifier that other people can cite. Zenodo can read only public repositories. Take about 7 minutes.

  1. Type git status. Make sure that all changes are committed.
  2. Type these commands:
git tag -a v1.0 -m "Version behind the draft and figure 1"
git push
git push --tags
gh release create v1.0 --title "v1.0" --notes "Version behind the Week 3 draft and figure 1. See review_log.md for how it got here."

You can also make the release on the web, in the Releases section of your repository on GitHub.

You should see

gh release create shows the web address of the release. The release page shows v1.0, your notes, and the source code as .zip and .tar.gz files. The repository is still Private.

Optional: an archived copy with a DOI

The GitHub integration of Zenodo archives a release and gives it a DOI. But "Zenodo can only access public repositories" (GitHub Docs, Referencing and citing content). Archive now only if the code and the data may be public. Read data/README.md. Ask your advisor. A public repository shows its full history, also every prompt in prompt-log.jsonl. Read that file first. If the project must stay private, archive it later, for example when your paper is accepted.

If the project may be public: make the repository public. Then follow the GitHub page above to turn on the repository in Zenodo. Zenodo archives each release that you make after that. Thus make a new release, for example v1.0.1. Add its DOI to README.md.

Lab 4

AI rules, from policy to your own

pairs, then individualno AI

Goal: apply the real rules of journals, funders, and UW–Madison to eight situations. Then write the rules that you will follow in your next project.

Sort eight situations

What and why

AI policies come in layers. Your university decides which data may go into which tools. Funders and journals add their own rules. The rules differ between journals, and they change often, so every summary can be out of date, also this one. When you can name the specific rule, you can defend your choice to an editor, a program officer, or a co-author. Take about 12 minutes, with the answers.

  1. With your partner, decide for each situation: allowed, allowed with disclosure, or not allowed.
  2. Name the rule for each decision. Use the policy pages in AI rules and data, and the page of your own target journal.
  3. If the answer depends on the journal, say which journal.
  4. Do not ask Claude to fill in the table. The point is that you find and read each rule yourself.
Worksheet
| # | Situation | Verdict | Which rule |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Claude fixes grammar and spelling in your manuscript for Geoscientific Model Development | | |
| 2 | Claude drafts the Discussion of your paper for an ESA journal; you edit it | | |
| 3 | The same Discussion, for Geoscientific Model Development | | |
| 4 | You upload a manuscript that you are reviewing to Claude, to summarize it | | |
| 5 | Claude writes the Specific Aims page of your NIH application | | |
| 6 | You paste the unpublished field data of your lab into your personal Claude account | | |
| 7 | AI touches up a field photo that is a figure in an Elsevier journal | | |
| 8 | Claude makes the slides for your talk at the ESA Annual Meeting, from your own published paper | | |

Write your own AI-use rules

What and why

Personal AI-use rules turn the experience of this month into decisions that you make before a deadline puts pressure on you. Make each rule specific, so that you can check it. Make at least one rule a guarantee: a deny rule, a hook, or a test. A guarantee works whatever the model decides. Write the rules without AI, so that they are yours. A hard question from your partner finds a rule that is only an intention. Your AI-use log shows what you really did, so update it first. Take about 8 minutes.

  1. Open your AI-use log. Add the missing rows for Week 3 and for Labs 1 and 2 of today.
  2. In your vault, make the file 40_Projects/my-ai-rules.md.
  3. Copy the template into it. Fill it in, without AI.
  4. Swap with your partner. Ask each other one hard question about a rule.

Course reflection

whole class

What and why

When you reflect on trust, ask how much you trust AI output now, and also why, and what changed it. Trust is most useful when it follows evidence. The evidence is the errors that you found, the checks that worked, and the tasks where AI helped or misled you. When you name a skill that could become weaker if you stopped checking, you start to guard against deskilling.

  1. Rate again. From 1 to 5, how much do you trust AI output for your research today? Compare it with your Week 1 exit ticket.
  2. Go around the room. Each person says three sentences. Say one practice that you will keep and one that you will stop. Then say one question about AI in research that you still cannot answer.
  3. Think. Which of your skills became stronger this month because you checked the AI? Which skills could become weaker if you stopped checking?
  4. Complete the course evaluation.

After the course

Suggested: the Week 4 practice. Finish what Lab 3 left open, such as licenses and a release. Write a plain-language summary and an AI-use statement for your own project. Package your workflow for your lab, revise your AI-use rules, and write a short reflection.
